On the die, the united matters undergo sublimation, the last fiery purification, from which the pure principles rise: the sel des philosophes (salt of the philosophers), «le roi couronné de gloire, qui prend naissance dans le feu» (the king crowned with glory, who is born in the fire), as in Hermes’ Rex ab igne veniet [PARIS.7 ¶19]. Only the king’s head shows above the flames, and Esprit Gobineau de Montluisant’s text says he wears a triple crown [PARIS.7 ¶19]. The residue then yields the volatile, fusible sel mercuriel, which old authors called Dragon babylonien [PARIS.7 ¶20]. The dragon emerges from the flames, its tail wrapping the human head, and grips the athanor with its claws [PARIS.7 ¶21]. In the flutings Fulcanelli reads a four-petalled flower for the universal matter, the quaternary of the first elements; below it the two natures whose union gives the Sages’ Saturne, «dénomination anagrammatique de natures» (an anagrammatic name for natures); four flutings for the second elements; and five units of the quintessence (three principles, two natures) totalling ten [PARIS.7 ¶22].

Against Cambriel, who denies that the multiplication of the Sulphur is shown, Fulcanelli points to the bands of touching circles on the dragon’s spine, wings and tail [PARIS.7 ¶23; PARIS.7 ¶24; PARIS.7 ¶25; PARIS.7 ¶26]. Their circles have a centre, the solar sign of gold, so the dragon, image of mercury, covered with the gold sign, can transmute in quantity, but only through later cookings with the Sulphur or philosophic Gold, which are the multiplications [PARIS.7 ¶27]. He agrees with Cambriel in general but not in extending the symbolism to the statue [PARIS.7 ¶28]. The science, he insists, is as exact as optics or geometry and must be cleansed of the frauds of puffers and charlatans [PARIS.7 ¶29]. Cambriel claimed the bishop lays a finger on his lips for silence, and his engraved plate is faked: bearded saint, short crozier, the dragon gnawing his foot [PARIS.7 ¶30; PARIS.7 ¶31]. The real statue is beardless, its mitre plain, its crozier set on the dragon’s mouth, and the saint blesses with index and middle finger raised [PARIS.7 ¶32]. The trumeau shows only the legend of Saint Marcel killing the dragon with his crozier; that it symbolises the life of matter is Cambriel’s opinion, but the gesture of silence «n’est pas et ne fut jamais» (is not and never was) [PARIS.7 ¶35].

PARIS.8 — Flamel’s buildings, the escutcheon of Saint Thomas, the Sainte-Chapelle

The great cathedrals, built by the medieval Frimasons to hand on the hermetic symbols and doctrine, influenced many humbler buildings [PARIS.8 ¶1]. Flamel covered his buildings with emblems: the small portal of Saint-Jacques-la-Boucherie (1389), with an angel holding a black marble round inlaid with a cross of gold thread; two houses for the poor (1407, 1410) with many carved figures; the chapel of the Saint-Gervais hospital; and two arcades at the charnier des Innocents (1389, 1407) [PARIS.8 ¶2]. On the first arcade was an escutcheon which, after Albert Poisson, Flamel imitated from one attributed to saint Thomas d’Aquin, and which appears at the end of Lagneau’s Harmonie Chymique [PARIS.8 ¶2; PARIS.8 ¶3]. Fulcanelli concludes that Lagneau copied it from the arcade, but holds the original escutcheon to be clearer; it was on a window of the chapel of Saint-Thomas-d’Aquin at the Jacobins’ convent until the Revolution [PARIS.8 ¶4]. It was drawn and painted from the window in 1787 by a hermetist named Chaudet, and that drawing is his source (pl. XXXI) [PARIS.8 ¶5]. It shows an upturned gold matras ringed with a crown of thorns; a gold cross with three blue globes and a red heart bearing a green branch, on which silver tears from the matras gather and fix; and quarters with stars and rays, a black earth with golden ears of wheat, a violet cloud with arrows darting toward the abyss, and three silver serpents on green [PARIS.8 ¶6]. This emblem, he says, reveals the extraction of the mercury and its conjunction with the sulphur, points on which all authors kept silent [PARIS.8 ¶7].

The Sainte-Chapelle of Pierre de Montereau (1245–1248) also held a remarkable alchemical ensemble; its original portal with two angels, one with a hand in a vase, the other in a cloud, is gone, but its south windows survive intact [PARIS.8 ¶8]. From the fifth bay Fulcanelli gives one panel, the Massacre of the Innocents, whose meaning he gave above (pl. XXXII), and he urges students to study the windows of the upper chapel and the great rose [PARIS.8 ¶8].

Amiens

AMIENS — The central porch as a copy of Paris

Fulcanelli notes that the central porch of Notre-Dame d’Amiens, the porch of the Saviour, reproduces almost faithfully both the motifs of the Paris portal and their order [AMIENS ¶1]. Only small details differ: at Paris the figures hold discs, here shields; the emblem of mercury is carried by a woman at Amiens and by a man at Paris [AMIENS ¶1]. He has no doubt that the hermetic work of Guillaume le Parisien influenced the decoration of the Amiens porch [AMIENS ¶1]. The building, due to Robert de Luzarches and Thomas and Renault de Cormont, was well enough preserved for restorers to respect most of its subjects [AMIENS ¶2].

AMIENS — The wheel fire (pl. XXXIII)

The first allegory proper to Amiens is a rendering of the feu de roue (“wheel fire”): a seated philosopher, leaning on his right knee, seems to meditate or keep watch beside two wheels (pl. XXXIII) [AMIENS ¶3; AMIENS ¶4]. Jourdain and Duval, Ruskin, the abbé Roze and G. Durand read it as Ezekiel’s vision of wheels within wheels, the prophet asleep on his knee [AMIENS ¶4]. Fulcanelli rejects this on two counts [AMIENS ¶5]: the carvers marked supernatural things with a border of clouds, which is absent here; the figure’s eyes are open; and a visionary is always shown facing the apparition in surprise or ecstasy, which this man is not [AMIENS ¶6]. The wheels therefore veil something well known to the initiate [AMIENS ¶6]. The man watches, patient but a little weary: the labours of Hercules done, his work is reduced to the «ludus puerorum des textes, c’est-à-dire à l’entretien du feu» (“children’s play” of the texts, that is, the keeping of the fire), which a woman spinning could do [AMIENS ¶6].

He reads the double wheel as «le signe de deux révolutions qui doivent agir successivement sur le composé pour lui assurer un premier degré de perfection» (the sign of two revolutions that must act in succession on the compound to give it a first degree of perfection); alternatively, as Pernety holds, the two natures in the conversion, also done by gentle, regular cooking [AMIENS ¶6]. In fact «la coction linéaire et continue exige la double rotation d’une même roue» (linear and continuous coction requires the double rotation of one same wheel), which stone cannot show, hence two interlocked wheels [AMIENS ¶7]. The first turn is the moist phase, élixation, in which the compound stays melted until a thin skin forms and thickens; the second, the dry phase or assation, ends when the contents of the egg appear calcined, granular or powdery, as crystals, sand or ash [AMIENS ¶7].

On this operation, «véritablement le sceau du Grand Œuvre» (truly the seal of the Great Work), an anonymous commentator says the philosopher cooks gently, in one vessel, «une seule vapeur qui s’épaissit peu à peu» (a single vapour that thickens little by little) [AMIENS ¶8; AMIENS n2]. On the heat: modern authors say it should start no higher than body temperature; Albert Poisson gives 50° rising to about 300° [AMIENS ¶8]. Philalèthe counts as a «chaleur tempérée» (tempered heat) one that keeps lead (327°) or tin (232°) molten, or more, as far as the vessels bear without breaking; from there one starts the heat proper to the kingdom where nature left the matter [AMIENS ¶8]. In his fifteenth rule, insisting the artist work on mineral not organic bodies [AMIENS ¶8], he has the water of «notre lac» (our lake) boil with the ashes of the tree of Hermes, night and day without cease, so that the celestial nature may rise and the terrestrial descend: «Car je vous assure que si nous ne faisons bouillir, nous ne pouvons jamais nommer notre ouvrage une cuisson, mais une digestion» (for I assure you that if we do not boil, we can never call our work a cooking, but a digestion) [AMIENS ¶9].

AMIENS — The “apostate” as the athanor (pl. XXXIV)

A small subject on the right of the same porch is read by Durand, with the abbé Roze, as Apostasy (Jourdain and Duval: Inconstancy), a replica of the seventh Paris medallion: a tonsured cleric throws down his breeches and shoes and walks away from a little church with a cylindrical, overhanging belfry (pl. XXXIV) [AMIENS ¶10; AMIENS ¶11]. Durand adds parallels at Paris and Chartres, where the figure wears only his shirt [AMIENS ¶11].

Fulcanelli sees no link with the Paris motif: that one shows the start of the Work, this one its completion [AMIENS ¶12]. «L’église est plutôt un athanor» (The church is rather an athanor), and its belfry, built against all rules of architecture, is «le four secret renfermant l’oeuf philosophal» (the secret furnace holding the philosophical egg), with openings to watch the phases [AMIENS ¶12]. The overlooked detail is the hollow arch in the base: it is hard to accept that a church would be built on open vaults, as if on four feet [AMIENS ¶12]. Nor is the soft mass the man points at easily taken for a garment; so Fulcanelli reads the motif as hermetic, showing the coction and its apparatus: the alchemist points with his right hand at the coal sack, and his discarded shoes show «jusqu’où doivent être poussés la prudence et le souci du silence dans cette besogne cachée» (how far prudence and care for silence must be carried in this hidden task) [AMIENS ¶12]. The Chartres figure’s light dress is explained by the furnace heat: at the fourth degree of fire, by the dry way, about 1200° must be kept, as also in projection [AMIENS ¶12]. He asks the authors why apostates should strip when leaving the temple [AMIENS ¶12]. As at Paris, the athanor takes the form of a turret on vaults; it shows the parts of the alchemical furnace, «cendrier, tour et dôme» (ash-pit, tower and dome), and old prints such as the woodcuts in Jean Liébaut’s treatise draw furnaces as keeps and small fortresses [AMIENS ¶13; AMIENS n4].

AMIENS — The cock and the fox (pl. XXXV)

On the pier of the great porch, a cock perches on an oak branch that a fox tries to reach (pl. XXXV), an allegory dear to Basile Valentin [AMIENS ¶14]. Profane readers see a medieval fable; Durand notes the dogs of the fable are missing, and Fulcanelli argues the exact old carvers would not have left them out had it been a fable [AMIENS ¶14].

He develops it further than at Paris: the cock is to the oak as son to father [AMIENS ¶15]. «le coq et le renard ne sont qu’un même hiéroglyphe recouvrant deux états physiques distincts d’une même matière» (the cock and the fox are only one hieroglyph covering two distinct physical states of one same matter) [AMIENS ¶15]. The cock is the volatile part extracted from the subject, whose emblem is the oak: the spring at the foot of the sacred tree, which the old philosophers called Mercury though it looks nothing like common quicksilver: «Car l’eau dont nous avons besoin est sèche, ne mouille pas les mains et jaillit sous le choc de la verge d’Aaron» (for the water we need is dry, does not wet the hands, and springs at the stroke of Aaron’s rod) [AMIENS ¶15]. The mercury contains everything the work needs, as Geber’s praise of it confirms; it is the only matter needed [AMIENS ¶15]. The cock, emblem of Mercury among the pagans and of the resurrection among Christians, can become the Phénix, but must first take the provisional fixity signified by the goupil, the hermetic fox [AMIENS ¶15].

Held long enough in the fire by the right means, this dry water becomes fixed and takes the fox as its sign: «L’eau est devenue terre et le mercure soufre» (The water has become earth and the mercury sulphur) [AMIENS ¶15]. But by an old axiom «toute teinture sèche est inutile en sa siccité» (every dry tincture is useless in its dryness), so the earth is redissolved in the water that bore it, «dans son propre sang» (in its own blood), and the fox takes back the cock’s wings [AMIENS ¶15]. A second coagulation follows, this time resisting the fire in fusion; this gives the first stone, fairly permanent in fire, very penetrating and fusible, qualities raised by a third reiteration [AMIENS ¶15]. Then the cock, attribute of Saint Peter, the true and flowing pierre (stone) on which the Christian edifice rests, «le coq aura chanté trois fois» (the cock will have crowed three times); Peter holds «deux clefs entrecroisées de la solution et de la coagulation» (two crossed keys of solution and coagulation), is the symbol of the volatile stone that fire makes fixed and dense by precipitating it, and was crucified head downward [AMIENS ¶15].

AMIENS — The citadel, the dead trees and the first agent (pl. XXXVI)

On the north (Saint-Firmin) portal, a bolted citadel with crenellated towers and a barred vent [AMIENS ¶16] may be the symbol of the esotericism of the 115 other quatrefoils, «l’idée mère de la Forteresse alchimique, reprise et modifiée par Khunrath en 1609» (the parent idea of the Alchemical Fortress, taken up and modified by Khunrath in 1609), or the closed Palace of the king of the Art of Basile Valentin and Philalèthe [AMIENS ¶17]. Below it, dead trees twist under a sky still showing sun, moon and stars (pl. XXXVI) [AMIENS ¶19]. These are the first matters, «planètes métalliques dont le feu, nous disent les Philosophes, a causé la mort, et que la fusion a rendues inertes» (metallic planets whose death, the Philosophers tell us, was caused by fire, and which fusion has left inert), like trees in winter [AMIENS ¶20]. Hence the Masters’ advice to réincruder them, giving back with fluid form «l’agent propre qu’elles ont perdu dans la réduction métallurgique» (the proper agent they lost in metallurgical reduction) [AMIENS ¶21].

Where to find this agent is the great mystery, which Fulcanelli says he has scattered across the emblems for the perceptive seeker, never mixing in false statements as the old authors did, and using the cabala to say what his predecessors did not, keeping silence where his oath forbade more [AMIENS ¶21]. Before the Secret des Secrets, the Verbum dimissum that Saint Paul says was hidden from all ages [AMIENS ¶22; AMIENS ¶23], he cites the masters [AMIENS ¶24]. «Le Chaos métallique produit des mains de la nature contient en soy tous les métaux et n’est point métail» (The metallic Chaos produced by nature’s hands contains all metals and is not metal) [AMIENS ¶25; AMIENS n6]. Haymon sends the seeker «à la partie postérieure du monde, là où l’on entend gronder le tonnerre, souffler le vent, tomber la grêle et la pluie» (to the hind part of the world, where thunder rumbles, wind blows, hail and rain fall) [AMIENS ¶26]. The commentator of the Lumière sortant des Ténèbres says Ægidius de Vadis shows that there remains in this world «une portion de ce premier Chaos, connue, mais méprisée d’un chacun, et qui se vend publiquement» (a portion of that first Chaos, known but despised by everyone, and sold publicly); the same commentator says the subject is to be sought in the mineral kingdom only [AMIENS ¶28]. Nicolas Valois’s stone «est dite pierre et n’est pas pierre» (is called stone and is not stone) [AMIENS ¶29]; Flamel’s is hidden in a fountain, «vile, abjecte et nullement prisée» (vile, abject and not at all prized) [AMIENS ¶30]; Le Breton names a mineral «lequel contient abondamment le fixe et le volatil» (which contains the fixed and the volatile in abundance) [AMIENS ¶31]. An anonymous author says the Philosophers rightly hid the mystery from those who value things only by their use, for if the Matter, which God hid in things that seem useful to them, were openly shown them, they would no longer value it [AMIENS ¶32].

AMIENS — The celestial dew, the Nostoc and the night (pl. XXXVII)

On the porch of the Virgin-Mother, an Adept watches «la rosée céleste» (the celestial dew) fall on a mass that many take for a fleece; Fulcanelli allows it may be the mineral called Magnésie or philosophical Aimant (Magnet), since the water falls on it alone, showing a hidden «vertu attractive» (attractive virtue) (pl. XXXVII) [AMIENS ¶34]. The relief thus renders «la condensation de l’esprit universel» (the condensation of the universal spirit) [AMIENS ¶34].

He corrects the error of puffers who took the Nostoc literally [AMIENS ¶35]. This green, jelly-like growth appears suddenly, swells with night dew and vanishes in the sun; these traits made the Philosophers take it as «type hiéroglyphique de leur matière» (hieroglyphic type of their matter), and the mass in the Amiens quatrefoil is such a heap, symbol of the Sages’ mineral Magnésie [AMIENS ¶35]. Its many names (Archée céleste, Crachat de Lune, Beurre de terre, Graisse de rosée, Vitriol végétal, Flos coeli) meant only their mineral principle [AMIENS ¶35]. The word comes from Greek νυξ, νυχτος, Latin nox, night: «C’est donc une chose qui naît la nuit, a besoin de la nuit pour se développer et ne peut se travailler que la nuit» (It is therefore a thing born at night, which needs the night to develop and can be worked only at night); so notre sujet is hidden from profane eyes, though easily recognised and worked by those who know the laws of nature exactly [AMIENS ¶36].

Since the artist seeks a generation, not a creation, the primary condition is «l’absence totale de toute lumière solaire, même diffuse ou tamisée» (the total absence of all sunlight, even diffuse or filtered) [AMIENS ¶37]. He argues from gestation, seeds, night dew, mushrooms, sleep and digestion, eggs hatched in light dying, and from minerals: silver salts decomposing in light, chlorine and hydrogen combining in it, ferrous sulphate turning ferric [AMIENS ¶37]. So «le soleil est le destructeur par excellence de toutes les substances trop jeunes, trop faibles pour résister à son pouvoir igné» (the sun is the destroyer par excellence of all substances too young, too weak to resist its fiery power), as phototherapy shows [AMIENS ¶38]. Work by day if you like, he says; Isis is mother of all things and alone dispenses Revelation and Initiation; one reaches Jesus only through his Mother, and the Virgin stands on the lunar crescent, dressed in blue, colour of the night star [AMIENS ¶39].

AMIENS — The star (pl. XXXVIII)

Left of the same porch, a master shows three disciples the astre hermétique, the traditional star that guides the Philosophers and announces the birth of «fils du soleil» (son of the sun) (pl. XXXVIII) [AMIENS ¶40]. He recalls Nicolas Rollin’s rebus device, painted in 1477 on the floor of the Beaune hospital, which showed its owner’s science by the sign of the Work, «la seule étoile» (the only star) [AMIENS ¶40].

Bourges

BOURGES.1 — The Jacques-Cœur palace

Besides its cathedral, Bourges has two remarkable buildings: the Jacques-Cœur palace and the Hôtel Lallemant [BOURGES.1 ¶1]. The palace was once «un véritable musée d’emblèmes hermétiques» (a true museum of hermetic emblems) [BOURGES.1 ¶2]. Vandalism and its successive uses have ruined the interior, and only the façade is still in its first state [BOURGES.1 ¶2].

Jacques Cœur, grand argentier of Charles VII, who built the palace in the fifteenth century, had the reputation of a proven Adept [BOURGES.1 ¶3]. David de Planis-Campy credits him with «le don précieux de la pierre au blanc» (the precious gift of the stone at the white), which Fulcanelli glosses as the transmutation of base metals into silver; this may be the source of the title argentier [BOURGES.1 ¶3]. Fulcanelli reads the motto «A vaillans cuers riens impossible» (To valiant hearts nothing impossible) by cabalistic rules [BOURGES.1 ¶4]. Read as cuer in the spelling of the period, it yields three things: the universal Spirit (rayon de lumière, “ray of light”); the common name of the basic matter worked (le fer, “iron”); and the three reiterations needed to perfect the two Magisteries (les trois cuers) [BOURGES.1 ¶4]. He concludes that Jacques Cœur practised alchemy, or at least watched the white stone being made «par le fer “essencifié” et trois fois cuit» (by “essencified” iron cooked three times) [BOURGES.1 ¶4].

The scallop shell and the heart always appear together on the palace [BOURGES.1 ¶5]. They may be a rebus on the owner’s name, but the scallop was also the pilgrims’ badge of Saint-Jacques [BOURGES.1 ¶5]. Fulcanelli says the Mérelle de Compostelle (pl. XXXIX) «sert, dans la symbolique secrète, à désigner le principe Mercure» (serves, in the secret symbolism, to designate the principle Mercury), which is also called the Voyageur (Traveller) or Pèlerin (Pilgrim) [BOURGES.1 ¶5]. A footnote adds that Mercury is the Philosophers’ «eau benoîte» (blessed water), and that large shells were once used to hold holy water [BOURGES.1 n1]. Everyone who undertakes the work and seeks the star (compos stella) wears the shell mystically [BOURGES.1 ¶5]. Flamel, in his Figures Hiéroglyphiques, describes the same allegorical journey to ask «Monsieur Jacques de Galice» for help [BOURGES.1 ¶5]. Every alchemist must make this journey, half by land and half by sea: «Pèlerins d’abord, pilotes ensuite.» (Pilgrims first, pilots afterwards.) [BOURGES.1 ¶5]

The restored chapel keeps nothing of the old symbolism except a ceiling of angels and a sculpted Annunciation [BOURGES.1 ¶6]. The most curious piece is a corbel in the Trésor room, said to show Tristan and Yseult (pl. XL) [BOURGES.1 ¶7]. Fulcanelli accepts the subject. He holds that the Round Table romances are traditional hermetic legends, renewed from the Greek fables, that pass on ancient science under the veil of fiction [BOURGES.1 ¶7]. At the centre, a hollow cubic casket sits at the foot of a leafy tree that hides King Marc’s crowned head; Tristan and Yseult stand on either side, gazing at the hollowed stone between them [BOURGES.1 ¶8].

For Fulcanelli, the Tristan myth repeats that of Theseus: Tristan kills the Morhout as Theseus kills the Minotaur [BOURGES.1 ¶9]. It is «l’hiéroglyphe de fabrication du Lion vert» (the hieroglyph of the making of the Green Lion), whence Tristan’s name Léonois; Basile Valentin teaches the same thing as the fight between the eagle and the dragon [BOURGES.1 ¶9]. This fight is «Ce combat singulier des corps chimiques dont la combinaison procure le dissolvant secret (et le vase du composé)» (This single combat of the chemical bodies whose combination yields the secret solvent (and the vessel of the compound)) [BOURGES.1 ¶9]. Fulcanelli lists its other forms: Cadmos, Apollo and Python, Jason, Horus and Typhon, Hercules and the Hydra, Perseus, saints Michael, George and Marcel, the fox and the cock of the Paris medallions, Cyliani’s alchemist and dragon, and the red and green serpents [BOURGES.1 ¶9].

The solvent allows the réincrudation of natural gold: its softening and its return to its first state, in a saline, friable and very fusible form [BOURGES.1 ¶10]. A footnote defines the term as «rendre cru, c’est-à-dire remettre dans un état antérieur à celui qui caractérise la maturité, rétrograder» (to make raw, that is, to put back into a state earlier than the one that marks maturity, to go backwards) [BOURGES.1 n2]. This is the rejuvenation of the king, personified here by Tristan, King Marc’s nephew; chemically, uncle and nephew are one and the same thing [BOURGES.1 ¶10]. Gold loses its crown when it loses its colour, and later gains a second one «infiniment plus noble que la première» (infinitely nobler than the first), as Limojon de Saint-Didier says [BOURGES.1 ¶10]. Hence the old king stays hidden in the tree that grows out of the stone [BOURGES.1 ¶10]. The queen is wife to both the old man and the young hero, which keeps «la tradition hermétique qui fait du roi, de la reine et de l’amant la triade minérale du Grand Œuvre» (the hermetic tradition that makes the king, the queen and the lover the mineral triad of the Great Work) [BOURGES.1 ¶10]. The tree behind Tristan is so loaded with huge fruit that Fulcanelli would compare the forest of Mort-Roi to the Garden of the Hesperides [BOURGES.1 ¶10].

BOURGES.2 — The Hôtel Lallemant

The “kitchen” and the vessel

The Hôtel Lallemant is perfectly preserved, with no restoration or damage to its symbolic decoration [BOURGES.2 ¶1]. Because it is built on a slope, a vaulted stairway leads up to the inner courtyard [BOURGES.2 ¶2]. The guide shows a low, dark basement room as the “kitchen”, pointing to a corbel of a clerk holding a pestle [BOURGES.2 ¶3]. Fulcanelli doubts this: the fireplace is too small to cook in but would hold the tower of an athanor [BOURGES.2 ¶3]. Such a room suits alchemical work, «d’où la lumière solaire, ennemie de toute génération, doit être exclue» (from which sunlight, the enemy of all generation, must be kept out) [BOURGES.2 ¶4]. No mortar is shown, and the “pestle” is really «un matras à long col» (a long-necked flask); its hollow, whistle-cut end proves that it is a vessel (pl. XLI) [BOURGES.2 ¶4].

This secret vessel bears names chosen to mislead the profane, usually œuf philosophique (philosophic egg) and Lion vert (Green Lion) [BOURGES.2 ¶5]. The text states that by œuf the Sages mean their compound placed in its own vessel; it holds the philosophic rebis, made of white and red as a bird’s egg is [BOURGES.2 ¶5]. No text has ever explained Lion vert [BOURGES.2 ¶5]. Batsdorff calls the cooking vessel the Green Lion without giving a reason [BOURGES.2 ¶5]. The Cosmopolite says that in the Work «il y a ce seul Lion verd qui ferme et ouvre les sept sceaux indissolubles des sept esprits métalliques» (there is this one Green Lion which shuts and opens the seven indissoluble seals of the seven metallic spirits) [BOURGES.2 ¶5]. G. Aurach’s manuscript shows a glass flask half full of a green liquor, whose name gives its colour; this is Basile Valentin’s vitriol [BOURGES.2 ¶5]. The third figure of the Toison d’Or shows the same thing [BOURGES.2 ¶5]. Fulcanelli judges that Ripley comes closest to the truth: «Il n’entre qu’un seul corps immonde dans notre magistère ; les Philosophes l’appellent communément Lion vert. C’est le milieu ou moyen pour joindre les teintures entre le soleil et la lune.» (Only one unclean body enters our magistery; the Philosophers commonly call it Green Lion. It is the medium or means for joining the tinctures between the sun and the moon.) [BOURGES.2 ¶5]

So the vessel is considered in two ways: by its matter, as the vase de nature (vessel of nature), and by its form, as the vase de l’art (vessel of art) [BOURGES.2 ¶6]. The citations above concern its nature [BOURGES.2 ¶6]. As to form, the egg may be spherical or oval, made of clear glass without bubbles, thick enough to resist internal pressure, and with a neck that can easily be sealed at the enameller’s lamp [BOURGES.2 ¶6]. Some authors recommend verre de Lorraine, which a footnote describes as moulded glass with thick, even walls [BOURGES.2 ¶6; BOURGES.2 n2]. The laboratory and the vessel, where the Adept works and where nature acts, are the two certainties that make the house a philosophical dwelling [BOURGES.2 ¶7].

Saint Christopher

In the loggia, a painted stone relief shows Saint Christopher setting the Child Jesus down on the bank of the torrent, while a hermit with a lantern comes out of his hut at night (pl. XLII) [BOURGES.2 ¶8]. Fulcanelli sees an analogy between the giant who carries Christ and «la matière qui porte l’or (Χρυσοφορος)» (the matter that carries gold), which plays the same role in the Work [BOURGES.2 ¶9]. He transcribes the legend as Amédée de Ponthieu tells it after Jacques de Voragine, italicising the words that bear on the work, its conditions and its materials [BOURGES.2 ¶9].

In the legend, the dull-witted giant Offerus travels to serve the greatest king on earth [BOURGES.2 ¶10]. He leaves a king who fears the devil to serve the devil, a black chief leading horsemen dressed in red [BOURGES.2 ¶11]. When the devil fears a wayside cross, Offerus passes the cross alone and seeks Christ [BOURGES.2 ¶11]. A hermit tells him that Christ is served by prayer, fasting and vigils, and then sets him to carry travellers across a torrent that comes down from a high mountain [BOURGES.2 ¶11]. One night a child calls him three times; in mid-stream the waters rise and the child grows heavier [BOURGES.2 ¶12]. Offerus says «Il me semble que je porte le monde.» (It seems to me that I am carrying the world.), and the child answers that he carries «celui qui a fait le monde» (him who made the world) [BOURGES.2 ¶12]. Christ baptises him Christophe, and from then on he travels the earth teaching the word [BOURGES.2 ¶12].

The artist, guided by the scholar of hermetism who commissioned the work, added a wide belt at the giant’s abdomen, and this belt gives the figure its true esoteric meaning [BOURGES.2 ¶13]. A footnote says that, according to documents in the house’s archives, Jean Lallemant belonged to the alchemical Fraternity of the Chevaliers de la Table ronde [BOURGES.2 n4]. Fulcanelli says this is not taught, but he explains it because «un livre qui n’apprend rien est inutile et vain» (a book that teaches nothing is useless and vain) [BOURGES.2 ¶13].

The Sign and the Epiphany cake

The belt is marked with criss-crossed lines like those on the surface of the solvent when it has been canoniquement (canonically) prepared [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. This is the Signe (Sign), which all the Philosophers take as the outward mark of the purity of their mercurial substance [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. Fulcanelli repeats that «tout le travail de l’art consiste à évertuer ce mercure jusqu’à ce qu’il soit revêtu du signe indiqué» (the whole work of the art consists in energising this mercury until it is clothed with the said sign) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. The old authors called this sign «Sceau d’Hermes, Sel des Sages (Sel mis pour Scel)» (Seal of Hermes, Salt of the Sages (Salt put for Seal)), the mark and signature of the Almighty, the Star of the Magi, and the Pole Star [BOURGES.2 ¶14].

The pattern shows more clearly when gold is dissolved in mercury to bring it back to its first state as or enfant (child gold) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. That is why mercury, the loyal serviteur (loyal servant), is called the Fountain of Youth [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. After the dissolution, mercury «porte l’enfant, le Fils du Soleil, le Petit Roi (Roitelet), comme une mère véritable, puisqu’en effet l’or renaît dans son sein» (carries the child, the Son of the Sun, the Little King (Kinglet), like a true mother, since gold is indeed reborn in its womb) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. Fulcanelli cites Hermes’ Table d’Emeraude, with a gloss on the wind inside the quotation: «Le vent, — qui est le mercure ailé et volatil, — l’a porté dans son ventre» (The wind — which is the winged and volatile mercury — carried it in its belly) [BOURGES.2 ¶14].

Fulcanelli finds the secret version of this truth in the Gâteau des Rois shared at Epiphany, the feast of the Child shown to the Magi, whom a star guided [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. The galette is “signed” like the matter itself and contains the little figure called the baigneur (bather) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. That figure is the Child carried by Offerus; it is «l’or dans son bain, le baigneur» (gold in its bath, the bather), and it is also the bean, the clog, the cradle, the cross, and the fish «qui nage dans notre mer philosophique» (that swims in our philosophic sea), in the Cosmopolite’s phrase [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. Christ was sometimes shown with a fish’s tail, for example on a capital at Saint-Brisson-sur-Loire [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. Fulcanelli states: «Le poisson est l’hiéroglyphe de la pierre des Philosophes dans son premier état, parce que la pierre, comme le poisson, naît dans l’eau et vit dans l’eau» (The fish is the hieroglyph of the Philosophers’ stone in its first state, because the stone, like the fish, is born in water and lives in water) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. Allegories say to catch it with a net, an image of the criss-cross meshes on the Epiphany cakes; Pfau’s 1702 alchemical stove shows an angler [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. A footnote adds that avoir de la galette (to have cake) means to be rich, and that whoever finds the bean will be doubly king, by science and by fortune [BOURGES.2 n7].

At a friend’s house, Fulcanelli saw a cake marked with an oak instead of lozenges, with a porcelain fish in place of the baigneur: «ce poisson était une sole (lat. Sol, solis, le soleil)» (this fish was a sole (Lat. Sol, solis, the sun)) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. He says the Cosmopolite’s fish Echinéis is the oursin (sea urchin, echinus), the ourson (bear cub), the petite ourse (Little Bear), the constellation of the pole star [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. This is why Limojon de Saint-Didier tells seekers to steer «par la vue de l’étoile du nord» (by the sight of the north star) [BOURGES.2 ¶14].

Whoever finds this poisson royal (royal fish) in the cake is made king [BOURGES.2 ¶15]. The dolphin was a “royal fish”, and the king’s eldest son bore the title Dauphin; the fishermen of the Mutus Liber try to catch a dolphin, and dolphins appear on the Hôtel Lallemant’s corner turret, on a pillar capital and on the chapel credence [BOURGES.2 ¶15]. Fulcanelli holds that the Greek Ichtus of the Catacombs has the same origin [BOURGES.2 ¶15]. A Catacomb painting reproduced by Martigny shows a fish carrying a basket of loaves; the basket, woven of crossed strands, is the same hieroglyph as the cake; he also points to the cista of Bacchus, in which, Fr. Noel says, «était renfermé ce qu’il y avait de plus mystérieux» (was shut up what was most mysterious) [BOURGES.2 ¶15].

The pastry is feuilletée (flaky, “leaved”), with the baigneur set in it like a bookmark [BOURGES.2 ¶16]. Sendivogius says that prepared mercury is a stony, friable, leafy mass: «Si vous l’observez bien, dit-il, vous remarquerez qu’elle est toute feuilletée.» (If you observe it well, he says, you will notice that it is all in leaves.) [BOURGES.2 ¶16] Its crystalline plates lie like the leaves of a book; hence the names terre feuillée and livre aux feuillets [BOURGES.2 ¶16]. The first matter is therefore shown as a book, open when it has been worked and closed when it has only been taken from the mine [BOURGES.2 ¶16]. A book sealed with seven bands marks the seven successive operations that open it, each one breaking a seal; this is the Great Book of Nature, easy to read if one can find it and open it [BOURGES.2 ¶16].

The chapel and the Golden Fleece

The chapel’s ornament is entirely profane and taken from hermetic science: a painted relief of the Golden Fleece, a coffered ceiling of hieroglyphic figures, and a credence holding an alchemical riddle [BOURGES.2 ¶17; BOURGES.2 ¶18]. Fulcanelli leaves open whether it was a chapel, a study or an oratory: «Chapelle, studio ou oratoire ?» (Chapel, study or oratory?) [BOURGES.2 ¶18]. The relief shows an oak forest in a ring of rocks, animals, reeds, and the ram’s fleece on a rock guarded by a dragon; Jason, once carved at the foot of an oak, has broken off (pl. XLIII) [BOURGES.2 ¶19].

For Fulcanelli the fable is a complete riddle of the hermetic work [BOURGES.2 ¶20]. The text states: «on appelle Toison d’or la matière préparée pour l’Œuvre, ainsi que le résultat final» (the prepared matter for the Work, as well as the final result, is called Golden Fleece) [BOURGES.2 ¶20]. The two differ only in purity, fixity and maturity: the Stone of the Philosophers is raw, while the Philosophers’ Stone that comes from it is fully cooked and digested [BOURGES.2 ¶20]. The task is not impossible, for «quiconque connaît le dragon sait aussi comment le vaincre» (whoever knows the dragon also knows how to overcome it); the real difficulty is interpreting the symbols, and Fulcanelli urges disciples to work at this material and tangible point [BOURGES.2 ¶20].

Here the oak and the ram are «une même chose sous deux aspects différents» (one same thing under two different aspects) [BOURGES.2 ¶21]. The old authors used the oak to «désigner le nom vulgaire du sujet initial, tel qu’on le rencontre dans la mine» (designate the common name of the initial subject, as it is found in the mine), by a near-pun whose equivalent answers to chêne [BOURGES.2 ¶21]. Fulcanelli says he cannot speak more plainly without overstepping certain limits, and dismisses the “doctors of kabbalah” [BOURGES.2 ¶21].

The oak bears galls (galla); bringing together «galla, Gallia, gallus, nous obtenons galle, Gaule, coq» (galla, Gallia, gallus, we obtain gall, Gaul, cock) [BOURGES.2 ¶22]. The cock is the emblem of Gaul and, as Jacob Tollius says, the attribute of Mercury [BOURGES.2 ¶22]. The oak also gives Kermès, «qui a, dans la Gaye Science, la même signification que Hermès, les consonnes initiales étant permutantes» (which has, in the Gay Science, the same meaning as Hermes, the initial consonants being interchangeable); both terms mean Mercury [BOURGES.2 ¶22]. The galle gives the name of the raw mercurial matter, while the kermès (Arabic girmiz, “which dyes scarlet”) marks the prepared substance [BOURGES.2 ¶22]. The text states that «le mercure des Philosophes, c’est-à-dire leur matière préparée, doit posséder la vertu de teindre» (the mercury of the Philosophers, that is, their prepared matter, must have the power to tinge), which it gains only through first preparations [BOURGES.2 ¶22].

The raw subject is called Magnesia lunarii, Plomb des Sages (Lead of the Sages), Saturnie végétable, and Fils or Enfant de Saturne (Son or Child of Saturn) by Philalèthe, Basile Valentin and the Cosmopolite [BOURGES.2 ¶23]. These names refer now to its magnet-like attraction for sulphur, now to its ready fusibility [BOURGES.2 ¶23]. All call it the Terre sainte (Holy Land), and its celestial hieroglyph is the sign of the Ram (Aries) [BOURGES.2 ¶23]. Gala is Greek for milk, and mercury is also called Lait de Vierge (Virgin’s Milk) [BOURGES.2 ¶23]. Fulcanelli says that whoever heeds what he said of the Epiphany cake, and knows why the Egyptians deified the cat, will know the subject’s common name; he does not state the name [BOURGES.2 ¶23]. «Ouvrez, c’est-à-dire décomposez cette matière» (Open, that is, break down this matter), isolate its pure part or “metallic soul”, and you will have the tinging mercury that carries the mystic gold, as Christopher carries Jesus and the ram its fleece [BOURGES.2 ¶23]. So the Fleece hangs on the oak like the gall and the kermes, and «le vieux chêne hermétique sert de mère au mercure secret» (the old hermetic oak serves as mother to the secret mercury) [BOURGES.2 ¶23].

The chapel ceiling

The ceiling’s coffers rest midway on two square pillars set against the walls [BOURGES.2 ¶24]. A footnote compares two other initiatic ceilings, at Dampierre-sur-Boutonne and at Plessis-Bourré [BOURGES.2 n12]. The right capital shows a winged human skull on oak leaves (pl. XLIV): «Traduction expressive d’une génération nouvelle, issue de cette putréfaction, consécutive à la mort, qui survient aux mixtes lorsqu’ils ont perdu leur âme vitale et volatile.» (Expressive rendering of a new generation, issued from that putrefaction, following death, which befalls compounds when they have lost their vital and volatile soul.) [BOURGES.2 ¶25] The dark blue or black colour of the dead body belongs to the «Corbeau, hiéroglyphe du caput mortuum de l’Œuvre» (Raven, hieroglyph of the caput mortuum of the Work), the first sign of dissolution and of the coming soufre (sulphur) [BOURGES.2 ¶25]. The wings teach that the loss of the volatile, watery part breaks the body’s cohesion; the body falls into black ash, which inner fire calcines until a pure sel (salt) is born [BOURGES.2 ¶25].

The left capital shows a vase flanked by two dolphins, with a flower like a heraldic lily; these refer to the solvent, or common mercury, the principle opposite to sulphur [BOURGES.2 ¶26]. At the base of both pillars, an oak-leaf crown crossed by a bundle of the same foliage is the graphic sign of the subject’s common name [BOURGES.2 ¶27]. Crown and capital together make the full symbol of the first matter: the globe held by God, Jesus and certain monarchs [BOURGES.2 ¶27].

Among the coffers (pl. XLV), Fulcanelli singles out several [BOURGES.2 ¶28]:

  • An armillary sphere on a fiery ground stands for sulphur and its extraction from the first matter [BOURGES.2 ¶28].
  • A beehive, a child urinating into his clog, and a child kneeling by flat ingots with an open book and a dead serpent [BOURGES.2 ¶28]. On the top ingot is Solomon’s starred seal: «En bas, le mercure ; en haut, l’Absolu.» (Below, mercury; above, the Absolute.) [BOURGES.2 ¶28] It is one way, one matter, one operation, and the most famous authors say: «Celui qui sait faire l’Œuvre par le seul mercure a trouvé ce qu’il y a de plus parfait.» (He who knows how to do the Work by mercury alone has found what is most perfect.) [BOURGES.2 ¶28] The two triangles of fire and water, sulphur and mercury joined in one body, make the six-pointed star, «hiéroglyphe de l’Œuvre par excellence et de la Pierre Philosophale réalisée» (hieroglyph of the Work par excellence and of the Philosophers’ Stone achieved) [BOURGES.2 ¶28].
  • A flaming forearm seizing chestnuts, and the same arm holding a torch out of a rock [BOURGES.2 ¶28].
  • Amalthea’s horn with a hen or partridge perched on it; Fulcanelli says a black hen or a red partridge would have the same meaning [BOURGES.2 ¶28].
  • A vase falling from a lion’s mouth, «c’est une version originale du solve et coagula de Notre-Dame de Paris» (it is an original version of the solve et coagula of Notre-Dame de Paris) [BOURGES.2 ¶28].
  • A child breaking a rosary over his knee [BOURGES.2 ¶28].
  • The mérelle with a bound mass on it; the coffer repeats fifteen times the graphic sign that identifies its content, and the same sign appears large in a furnace [BOURGES.2 ¶28].
  • The child, who seems to play the artist, standing in the shell and throwing small shells [BOURGES.2 ¶28].
  • An open book burning; a haloed dove, emblem of the Spirit; a fiery raven pecking a skull, for death and putrefaction; and an angel spinning the world like a top, as in the Jesuits’ Typus Mundi [BOURGES.2 ¶28].
  • Philosophic calcination, shown as a pomegranate in fire in a goldsmith’s vase, marked 3 R for the trois réitérations (three reiterations) of the same process [BOURGES.2 ¶28].
  • The ludus puerorum of Trismosin’s Toison d’or: a child on a hobby horse [BOURGES.2 ¶28].

The credence: RERE, RER

The small sixteenth-century credence by the window holds a riddle that no visitor had solved, according to the guide; Fulcanelli says this is because no one grasped the aim of the decoration or read the Golden Fleece relief in its true sense (pl. XLVI) [BOURGES.2 ¶30]. Its pilasters carry emblems of the mercure philosophal (philosophic mercury): the mérelle, or holy-water shell, with wings and Neptune’s trident, always signifying the watery, volatile principle [BOURGES.2 ¶30]. Its pediment is a shell with two dolphins tied together, and three flaming pomegranates [BOURGES.2 ¶30].

The riddle is two terms, RERE and RER, each repeated three times in the niche [BOURGES.2 ¶31]. The repetition signals three repetitions of one technique [BOURGES.2 ¶32]. The three pomegranates confirm this: they stand for the «feu corporifié dans ce sel rouge qu’est le Soufre philosophal» (fire made bodily in that red salt which is the philosophic Sulphur), whose calcination must be repeated three times to perform Geber’s three works [BOURGES.2 ¶32]. The first gives the Sulphur (first-order medicine); the second, identical, gives the Elixir, which differs from the Sulphur only in quality; the third gives the Philosophers’ Stone, which has all their virtues multiplied [BOURGES.2 ¶32]. For how this is done, Fulcanelli points to the ceiling relief of the pomegranate roasting in a vessel [BOURGES.2 ¶32].

Fulcanelli’s decoding of RERE: RE, the Latin ablative of res, means the thing, considered as matter; RE plus RE is “two things in one”, so RERE equals RE BIS, the REBIS that means the Philosophers’ compound, ready to be transformed by fire [BOURGES.2 ¶33]. «RE, une matière sèche, or philosophique ; RE, une matière humide, mercure philosophique» (RE, a dry matter, philosophic gold; RE, a wet matter, philosophic mercury); RERE is their amalgam, with a double hidden property in exact balance [BOURGES.2 ¶33].

Fulcanelli says he may not fully unveil RER, but that it holds a secret of the first importance about the vase de l’Œuvre (vessel of the Work): RER serves to cook and unite the compound RERE and to bring about its changes [BOURGES.2 ¶34]. He warns against trusting Basile Valentin’s Douze Clefs or taking literally the words «celui qui a la matière trouvera bien un pot pour la cuire» (whoever has the matter will surely find a pot to cook it in); he affirms that without perfect knowledge of the Philosophers’ Vessel and how to make it, no success is possible [BOURGES.2 ¶34]. Pontanus says he failed more than two hundred times before he knew this vessel [BOURGES.2 ¶34]. «L’artiste doit faire lui-même son vaisseau ; c’est une maxime de l’art.» (The artist must make his vessel himself; it is a maxim of the art.) [BOURGES.2 ¶34] Nothing should be undertaken before full light on this eggshell, which the medieval masters called secretum secretorum [BOURGES.2 ¶34].

Since RE is a thing, R, half of RE, is half a thing; RER is a matter increased by half of another matter or of itself, as a chemical combination, not a proportion [BOURGES.2 ¶35]. As an example only, Fulcanelli supposes RE to be réalgar (natural arsenic sulphide); RER would then be realgar increased by its sulphur or by its arsenic, each taken as half of it [BOURGES.2 ¶35].

His advice is to seek RER, the vessel, first; RERE will then be easy to know [BOURGES.2 ¶36]. Asked what a Philosopher is, the Sibyl answered: «C’est celui qui sait faire le verre.» (It is he who knows how to make glass.) [BOURGES.2 ¶36] The potter’s craft teaches more than glassmaking: one of Piccolpassi’s plates shows «une colombe dont les pattes sont attachées à une pierre» (a dove whose feet are tied to a stone) [BOURGES.2 ¶36]. Following Tollius, the magistery lies in something volatile, which needs a vessel to keep it from escaping [BOURGES.2 ¶36]. Fulcanelli’s procedure: make a vessel, then the compound; seal it so that no spirit escapes; heat until complete calcination; put the pure part of the powder back into the compound and seal it in the same vessel; repeat a third time [BOURGES.2 ¶36]. He asks no thanks, only that the reader take the same trouble for others [BOURGES.2 ¶36].

Jean Lallemant

The author of these emblems left no known book; like the great medieval Adepts, he preferred to entrust his science to stone rather than vellum [BOURGES.2 ¶37]. Fulcanelli names him: Jean Lallemant, alchemist and knight of the Round Table, worthy of a place beside Geber and Roger Bacon [BOURGES.2 ¶38]. He is equal in knowledge to Basile Valentin and Flamel and superior to them in two qualities: «la modestie et la sincérité» (modesty and sincerity) [BOURGES.2 ¶38].

The Cyclic Cross of Hendaye

HENDAYE — The cross of the end of the cycle

A plain stone cross beside the parish church of Hendaye, a small Basque border town, was moved there from the cemetery in 1842; its origin is unknown, and Fulcanelli dates it from its base and column to no earlier than the late seventeenth or early eighteenth century [HENDAYE ¶1; HENDAYE ¶2]. It is for him the strangest monument of primitive millenarianism, the rarest symbolic rendering of chiliasm he has met, a doctrine he places among the esoteric traditions of the philosophy of Hermès [HENDAYE ¶2]. The reliefs are naive and clumsily carved, not a professional’s work, yet their unknown maker, he holds, had deep science and real cosmographic knowledge [HENDAYE ¶3].

The crossbar of this Greek cross carries the common salutation to the cross as sole hope, cut on two lines so that the second begins with pes, “foot”, instead of spes, “hope”, which also breaks the grammar, since pes is masculine and its adjective feminine [HENDAYE ¶4; HENDAYE ¶5; HENDAYE ¶6; HENDAYE ¶7]. Because the other motifs are carefully placed and the letters are clean and do not overlap (pl. XLVII), Fulcanelli rejects a slip of the carver and takes the apparent error as a sign set there on purpose to rouse the observer’s curiosity [HENDAYE ¶7]. He says the pedestal had already given him the key, and he argues from the inscription only to show what common sense and logic can do [HENDAYE ¶8].

The serpent-shaped S answers to the Greek khi (X) and takes its esoteric meaning: the helical track of the sun at the zenith of its curve at the cyclic catastrophe, and an image of the beast of the Apocalypse, the dragon that pours fire and sulphur on creation at the Judgement [HENDAYE ¶9]. It tells him to read the line in the secret language, the language of the gods or of the birds, by the rules of Diplomatics, which he says Grasset d’Orcet set out clearly: read the Latin as written but in French, permute the vowels, and from the sounds rebuild a new sentence with its spelling and word order restored [HENDAYE ¶9]. The result is a warning: «Il est écrit que la vie se réfugie en un seul espace» (It is written that life takes refuge in a single space) [HENDAYE ¶9]. A note takes “space” in the sense of a place, a country or territory [HENDAYE n1]. So, Fulcanelli concludes, there is a land where death will not reach man at the time of the double cataclysm, and finding where it lies is left to us [HENDAYE ¶9]. From there the elect will see the golden age return; the elect, children of Elie, will be saved as Scripture says, raised by faith and perseverance to be disciples of the Christ-Light, will bear his sign and tie regenerated mankind back to the traditions of the vanished one [HENDAYE ¶9].

The front face bears INRI and corresponds to the cycle figure on the base (pl. XLVIII), so the monument holds two crosses: above, the divine cross, the means of expiation; below, the cross on the globe, marking the pole of the northern hemisphere and the time of that expiation [HENDAYE ¶10]. God the Father holds this globe topped with the fiery sign, and the sovereigns of the four great centuries, historical figures of the four ages of the world, are shown with the same attribute: Alexandre, Auguste, Charlemagne, Louis XIV [HENDAYE ¶10]. A note adds that the last was only a king, the Sun-King, marking the decline of the star before the long cyclic night [HENDAYE n2]. Read openly, INRI names Jesus of Nazareth, King of the Jews; taken from the cross, its secret sense is that nature is renewed whole by fire, for our hemisphere will soon be tried in fire, and the good will be parted from the wicked as fire parts gold from impure metals [HENDAYE ¶10].

The four faces of the pedestal show the sun, the moon, a great star, and the initiates’ figure of the solar cycle: a circle quartered by two perpendicular diameters, each quarter marked with an A for one of the four ages of the world [HENDAYE ¶11]. Fulcanelli calls it a complete hieroglyph of the universe, joining heaven and earth, macrocosm and microcosm, the cross of redemption and the circle of the world [HENDAYE ¶11]. Medieval art showed the four phases by the evangelists, the Greek alpha, or most often the four evangelical animals round Christ, the human and living figure of the cross, set apart from them by the mystic almond, as at Chartres, Le Mans, Luz, Civray and Saint Trophine d’Arles (pl. XLIX) [HENDAYE ¶12]. He cites saint Jean’s four animals before the throne, like a lion, a calf, a man and an eagle, and the matching account of Ezéchiel [HENDAYE ¶13]. Hindu myth sets on the quartered circle a cow, Virtue, standing on four feet in the first age, the Crédayougam, and on only one in the Calyougam, our iron age of misery and decrepitude [HENDAYE ¶14].

The iron age bears only the seal of Death: the skeleton with Saturn’s attributes, the empty hourglass of time run out and the scythe, echoed in the figure seven, number of transformation and destruction [HENDAYE ¶15]. Its Gospel is that of saint Matthieu, whose name Fulcanelli derives from the Greek for “science”: the Gospel according to Science, last of all but first for us, since it teaches that all but a few elect must perish together [HENDAYE ¶15]. Hence the angel given to saint Matthieu: science alone can give man wings to rise to the highest truths and reach God [HENDAYE ¶15].

Conclusion

CONCL — To know, to be able, to dare, to keep silent

The conclusion opens with a four-word motto signed ZOROASTRE, «Scire. Potere. Audere. Tacere.» (To know. To be able. To dare. To be silent.), and with the sentence: «La Nature n’ouvre pas à tous, indistinctement, la porte du sanctuaire.» (Nature does not open the door of the sanctuary to all without distinction.) [CONCL ¶1; CONCL ¶2; CONCL ¶3]. The disciple will profit if he studies the old Philosophers’ classic texts until he can discern «les points obscurs du manuel opératoire» (the obscure points of the operative manual) [CONCL ¶4]. He must tune his whole life to the search; zeal without sound principle, or ruled by pride or greed, is not enough [CONCL ¶5; CONCL ¶6]. The Science asks for exact observation, a sound mind, a lively but sober imagination, a pure heart, indifference to received theories, thinking with one’s own brain, and seeking its truth from Nature, «notre mère commune» (our common mother) [CONCL ¶7]. Observation and meditation lead to knowing; imitation of natural processes and long experience give power; patience, will and faith let him dare [CONCL ¶8; CONCL ¶9; CONCL ¶10; CONCL ¶11; CONCL ¶12; CONCL ¶13]. Once he succeeds, the Sage turns to the humble and the suffering, an anonymous disciple of Nature and apostle of Charity, faithful to his vow of silence: in Science and in Good the Adepte must forever «SE TAIRE» (keep silent) [CONCL ¶14; CONCL ¶15; CONCL ¶16].

The Book as a Whole

The thesis

Fulcanelli’s claim is that the Gothic cathedral is a book. It is one of the «beaux livres d’images dressés sur nos parvis» (fine picture books set up on our church squares) [INTRO.1 ¶1; INTRO.1 ¶2], and unlike written books it conveys a single, absolute meaning, simply expressed [INTRO.1 ¶4]. It is an encyclopedia of medieval knowledge. What draws him most in it is the symbolism of the old alchemists, which the medieval sculptors rendered skilfully [INTRO.2 ¶3; INTRO.2 ¶4]. Notre-Dame de Paris is his type [INTRO.9 ¶1]. Its central trumeau puts Alchemy in the place of honour, as «le sceau du Grand Oeuvre laïque au front du Grand Oeuvre chrétien» (the seal of the lay Great Work on the brow of the Christian Great Work) [PARIS.1 ¶3; PARIS.1 ¶4]. The cathedral, he says, is founded on alchemy. Stripped of her veil, the Virgin-Mother to whom it is dedicated personifies the primitive substance that the creating Principle used [PARIS.1 ¶5]. The whole building is a mute but pictured glorification of the science of Hermes [PARIS.1 ¶12]. Time, vandals and restorers have destroyed much, but enough survives to find the hidden arcanum again [INTRO.2 ¶7; INTRO.2 ¶8]. He holds that this science is as exact as optics or geometry [PARIS.7 ¶29]. The book thus does two things: it shows how to read the monuments, and it uses that reading to set out the Great Work; Paris closes on the paradox of «le flambeau de l’alchimique pensée illuminant le temple de la pensée chrétienne» (the torch of alchemical thought lighting the temple of Christian thought) [PARIS.5 ¶19; PARIS.5 ¶20].

How the parts build it

Canseliet’s prefaces (1925, 1957, 1964) [PREF1 ¶13; PREF2 ¶46; PREF3 ¶51] frame the book but are not part of its argument. The first says that, on the Author’s assurance, one of the book’s figures gives the key of the major arcanum, and that this key is a colour shown to the worker from the first labour [PREF1 ¶11]. The second explains the Master’s anonymity [PREF2 ¶2]. It also develops the double star [PREF2 ¶21; PREF2 ¶26] and credits the book with restoring the phonetic cabale [PREF2 ¶45]. The third defends the fertile putrefaction [PREF3 ¶15] and Fulcanelli’s correction of Cambriel over the statue of Saint Marcel [PREF3 ¶48].

The Introduction sets up the frame of reading. The church plan is a cross, the hieroglyph of the crucible [INTRO.4 ¶1]. The labyrinth stands for the whole labour of the Work [INTRO.5 ¶3]. The three roses display the colours, and the rose window stands for the fire and its duration [INTRO.6 ¶2; INTRO.6 ¶12]. The Star of the Magi shines on the compost [INTRO.6 ¶13]. The Black Virgins of the crypts stand for the primitive earth [INTRO.8 ¶4], and Saint Christopher is the one “who carries gold” [INTRO.9 ¶15].

Paris carries the main weight. At the base of the façade one follows the work of the Elixir from first calcination to final coction [PARIS.2 ¶1]. The sequence runs as follows:

  • the Fountain, or solvent, and the athanor [PARIS.2 ¶6; PARIS.2 ¶25];
  • twelve figures giving the black, Mercury, the secret fire, the colours, the conjunction and the Lions [PARIS.3; PARIS.4];
  • medallions on the Solution, which alone shows which of two ways is followed [PARIS.5 ¶14];
  • the north portal, on the universal Spirit made bodily, the May Dew [PARIS.6 ¶23; PARIS.6 ¶24];
  • the south portal, on the short dry way [PARIS.7 ¶1];
  • Flamel’s buildings and the Saint-Thomas escutcheon [PARIS.8 ¶7].

Amiens repeats the Paris porch almost exactly [AMIENS ¶1] and adds what Paris lacks:

  • the coction as the “wheel fire” [AMIENS ¶6; AMIENS ¶7];
  • the athanor at the end of the Work [AMIENS ¶12];
  • the cock and fox as one matter in two states [AMIENS ¶15];
  • the dead metals that must be revived (réincruder) [AMIENS ¶20; AMIENS ¶21];
  • the night as the condition of generation [AMIENS ¶37];
  • the star [AMIENS ¶40].

Bourges moves from church to house. Jacques Cœur’s palace yields the stone at the white and Mercury as pilgrim [BOURGES.1 ¶3; BOURGES.1 ¶5]. The Hôtel Lallemant yields:

  • the vessel [BOURGES.2 ¶4];
  • the Sign [BOURGES.2 ¶14];
  • the Epiphany cake [BOURGES.2 ¶14];
  • the Golden Fleece [BOURGES.2 ¶20];
  • the riddle RERE, RER [BOURGES.2 ¶33; BOURGES.2 ¶34].

Its author entrusted his science to stone rather than vellum [BOURGES.2 ¶37].

The later Hendaye chapter reads a cross no older than the late seventeenth century [HENDAYE ¶2]. It turns the same method from the Work to the cycle of the world: nature renewed whole by fire, and life taking refuge in a single place [HENDAYE ¶9; HENDAYE ¶10].

The Conclusion states what the worker must be [CONCL ¶7].

The threads

The key of reading

Fulcanelli reads by sound. «art gothique n’est qu’une déformation orthographique du mot argotique, dont l’homophonie est parfaite» (Gothic art is only a misspelling of the word argotique, whose homophony is perfect) [INTRO.3 ¶5]. Argot is a spoken cabala, and it derives from the Language of the Birds [INTRO.3 ¶5; INTRO.3 ¶8]. The task is to dissect words, break their bark and free their spirit [INTRO.3 ¶7]. The same rule gives several readings:

  • Christophe as Chrysophe [INTRO.9 ¶15];
  • Kermès as Hermès [BOURGES.2 ¶22];
  • RERE as REBIS [BOURGES.2 ¶33];
  • the Hendaye pes for spes [HENDAYE ¶7; HENDAYE ¶9].

Beside the cabala stands a rule of exact looking. At the Paris porch he declines two biblical subjects, to avoid any charge of arbitrary interpretation, and keeps to motifs whose profane character is beyond doubt [PARIS.2 ¶26]. He sets aside the Ezekiel reading at Amiens because of what the carving shows: no clouds and open eyes [AMIENS ¶6]. He holds that Saint Marcel’s gesture of silence “is not and never was” [PARIS.7 ¶35].

The matter

The first matter is a black, heavy, brittle ore from the mines, hence the black underground Virgins [INTRO.8 ¶4]. It is the rough cornerstone, often given the devil’s image [INTRO.4 ¶5; INTRO.4 ¶6]. The authors he quotes call it the Miroir de l’Art [PARIS.5 ¶4]. A commentator he cites says a portion of the first Chaos remains, despised by everyone and sold publicly, and that the subject is to be sought in the mineral kingdom only [AMIENS ¶28]. The oak names its common name [BOURGES.2 ¶21], but the name itself is never written [BOURGES.2 ¶23].

The solvent and the secret fire

The hidden Fountain is the solvent that penetrates all metals [PARIS.2 ¶6]. Its agent is a secret fire that looks more like a water than a flame. Fulcanelli calls it the highest secret of the Work and says it stopped him for more than twenty years [PARIS.3 ¶20]. It is the universal Spirit, which must be caught in an attractive body, the magnet in the belly of Aries [PARIS.4 ¶13; PARIS.4 ¶16], and condensed as May Dew [PARIS.6 ¶24]. The first magnetic agent used to prepare the solvent is the Green Lion [PARIS.4 ¶17]. With it, gold and the dead metals can be brought back to their first state [BOURGES.1 ¶10; AMIENS ¶21]. Theseus, Tristan, Saint George and Saint Marcel are all figures of the single combat that yields this solvent [BOURGES.1 ¶9].

The stages and signs

The essential colours follow a fixed order: black, white, red [PARIS.3 ¶25]. «Le Corbeau est, en quelque sorte, le sceau canonique de l’Œuvre, comme l’étoile est la signature du sujet initial» (The Raven is, as it were, the canonical seal of the Work, as the star is the signature of the initial subject) [PARIS.3 ¶3]. Yet Fulcanelli warns: «Ne vous en rapportez point trop à la couleur» (Do not trust too much to the colour) [PARIS.3 ¶33]. A regime is a way of feeding the stone’s life, which need not show as a sequence of colours [PARIS.3 ¶35]. Its main operations (the order of this list is ours, not the book’s):

  1. Sublimations. The first conjunction is made by repeated sublimations, the “eagles”; Philalèthe asks for no fewer than seven and up to nine [PARIS.4 ¶3].
  2. The Sign. The prepared mercury must bear the Sign, a criss-cross on its surface, which the old authors also called the Star of the Magi [BOURGES.2 ¶14]; compare the star on the compost [INTRO.6 ¶13].
  3. Coction. The coction is a wheel fire turning twice, first moist, then dry [AMIENS ¶7].
  4. Three repetitions. One technique, repeated three times, gives the Sulphur, the Elixir and the Stone [BOURGES.2 ¶32].
  5. Multiplications. Later cookings with the Sulphur, the multiplications, let the mercury transmute in quantity [PARIS.7 ¶27].

Through it all, as the Philosophers say, «le Mercure seul suffit» (Mercury alone suffices) [PARIS.3 ¶18; BOURGES.2 ¶28]. There are two ways. In the first, alchemical gold is dissolved by the Alkaest; in the second, common gold is dissolved by notre mercure [PARIS.5 ¶14]. The south portal sets a short dry way, worked in a crucible, against a longer wet way [PARIS.7 ¶3; PARIS.7 ¶4; PARIS.7 ¶7]. Generation needs total darkness [AMIENS ¶37]. Nothing succeeds without knowledge of the vessel [BOURGES.2 ¶34].

The worker

The ladder of nine rungs on the trumeau stands for patience [PARIS.1 ¶3]. Once the labours of Hercules are done, the work is “children’s play”, the keeping of the fire [AMIENS ¶6]. It also calls for prudence and silence [AMIENS ¶12]. The artist must make his own vessel [BOURGES.2 ¶34]. He is “Pilgrim first, pilot afterwards” [BOURGES.1 ¶5]. The Conclusion asks for:

  • exact observation and a sound mind;
  • a pure heart;
  • indifference to received theories;
  • seeking truth from Nature [CONCL ¶7].

Knowing comes from observation, power from imitation of Nature, and daring from patience, will and faith [CONCL ¶8–¶13]. The Adept who succeeds serves the humble and the suffering, and keeps silent [CONCL ¶14; CONCL ¶15; CONCL ¶16]. Fulcanelli praises Lallemant’s modesty and sincerity [BOURGES.2 ¶38]. He explains what he does because «un livre qui n’apprend rien est inutile et vain» (a book that teaches nothing is useless and vain) [BOURGES.2 ¶13].

What the book leaves unsaid

Fulcanelli says openly how he handles the great mystery. He has scattered the agent across the emblems for the perceptive seeker, never mixed in false statements, and kept silent where his oath forbade more [AMIENS ¶21]. Each open point is named as such:

  • He stops at the body that yields the May Dew, calling it the Verbum dimissum [PARIS.6 ¶25].
  • He will not reveal the secret fire [PARIS.3 ¶20].
  • He gives the subject’s common name only by hint (the cake, the cat), never in words [BOURGES.2 ¶23], and cannot speak more plainly of the oak [BOURGES.2 ¶21].
  • He may not fully unveil RER, the vessel [BOURGES.2 ¶34].
  • He leaves the Paris Lion undecided, since its paint is gone [PARIS.4 ¶19].
  • He leaves to the reader where the land of refuge lies [HENDAYE ¶9].

Canseliet adds two silences of his own. He does not say which figure gives the key of the colour [PREF1 ¶11]. And he holds that the Master’s real name stays hidden behind the pseudonym [PREF2 ¶2]. The book ends under the motto «Scire. Potere. Audere. Tacere.» (To know. To be able. To dare. To be silent.) and the sentence «La Nature n’ouvre pas à tous, indistinctement, la porte du sanctuaire.» (Nature does not open the door of the sanctuary to all without distinction.) [CONCL ¶1; CONCL ¶2; CONCL ¶3].

A: Reading the Stone Book — how Fulcanelli interrogates the monuments

This page states, for a modern reader, the method by which Le Mystère des Cathédrales reads the Gothic cathedrals and a few later buildings. It is organised by principle. Every claim is marked stated (the text says it) or interpreted (our inference, with the passages it rests on). Quotations are verbatim French with our English beside them. Canseliet’s prefaces are kept to the final section.

1. The claim: the cathedral is a book, and its text is alchemy

Stated. The first move of the book is to treat the cathedrals literally as books. They are «ces beaux livres d’images dressés sur nos parvis, et qui développent jusqu’au ciel leurs feuillets de pierre sculptés» (these beautiful picture-books raised on our forecourts, which unfold their sculpted stone leaves up to the sky) [INTRO.1 ¶2]. Their letters are carvings: «Si ces livres lapidaires ont leurs lettres sculptées, — phrases en bas-reliefs et pensées en ogives, — ils n’en parlent pas moins par l’esprit, impérissable, qui s’exhale de leurs pages» (If these lapidary books have their letters carved — sentences in bas-reliefs and thoughts in ogives — they speak nonetheless by the imperishable spirit that breathes from their pages) [INTRO.1 ¶4].

Stated. The stone book is held to be clearer than written ones, because it carries a single, absolute meaning, «un sens purgé des finesses, des allusions, des équivoques littéraires» (a meaning purged of the subtleties, allusions and equivocations of literature) [INTRO.1 ¶4]. This is the ground of the whole method: a carving is taken to mean one thing, not many (interpreted from the same passage).

Stated. The cathedral is not only Christian. It is «une vaste concrétion d’idées, de tendances, de foi populaires, un tout parfait auquel on peut se référer sans crainte dès qu’il s’agit de pénétrer la pensée des ancêtres» (a vast concretion of ideas, tendencies and popular faith, a perfect whole to which one may refer without fear whenever one wants to penetrate the thought of the ancestors) [INTRO.1 ¶6]. Its structure and ornament breathe «un esprit plus laïque et, disons le mot, presque païen» (a more secular, and let us say it, almost pagan spirit) [INTRO.1 ¶8]. It is «une encyclopédie très complète et très variée, tantôt naïve, tantôt noble, toujours vivante, de toutes les connaissances médiévales» (a very complete and varied encyclopaedia, now naive, now noble, always living, of all medieval knowledge), and its carvings are «des éducateurs, des initiateurs au premier chef» (educators, initiators of the first rank) [INTRO.2 ¶3].

Stated. Among all this, the part that matters to the book is «le symbolisme des vieux alchimistes, habilement traduit par les modestes statuaires médiévaux» (the symbolism of the old alchemists, skilfully translated by the modest medieval statuaries) [INTRO.2 ¶4]. Notre-Dame de Paris is called «église philosophale» (philosophal church), and Victor Hugo is quoted (QUOTED: Victor Hugo, endorsed) calling it «l’abrégé le plus satisfaisant de la science hermétique, dont l’église de Saint-Jacques-la Boucherie était un hiéroglyphe si complet» (the most satisfying abridgement of hermetic science, of which the church of Saint-Jacques-la-Boucherie was so complete a hieroglyph) [INTRO.2 ¶4].

Stated. The reading has precedents: the alchemists of the fourteenth century met weekly at Notre-Dame, «au jour de Saturne, soit au grand porche, soit au portail Saint-Marcel, ou encore à la petite Porte-Rouge» (on Saturn’s day, either at the great porch, or at the Saint-Marcel portal, or again at the little Red Door) [INTRO.2 ¶5]; there «on y étudiait sur place l’allégorie du beau livre» (one studied on the spot the allegory of the beautiful book), and the liveliest part of the meetings was «l’exégèse abstruse des mystérieux symboles» (the abstruse exegesis of the mysterious symbols) [INTRO.2 ¶6]. Fulcanelli places himself after Gobineau de Montluisant and Cambriel and sets out to «parler aux pierres et les interroger» (speak to the stones and question them) [INTRO.2 ¶7], to «retrouver le sens de l’arcane dissimulé sous l’écorce pétrifiée du prodigieux grimoire» (recover the meaning of the arcanum hidden under the petrified bark of the prodigious grimoire) [INTRO.2 ¶8].

Stated. The book’s own name for the cathedral is a silent book: the trumeau figure of alchemy is «le titre du chapitre de ce mutus Liber qu’est le temple gothique ; le frontispice de cette Bible occulte aux massifs feuillets de pierre» (the title of the chapter of this mutus Liber that is the Gothic temple; the frontispiece of this occult Bible with massive leaves of stone), «le sceau du Grand Oeuvre laïque au front du Grand Oeuvre chrétien» (the seal of the secular Great Work on the brow of the Christian Great Work) [PARIS.1 ¶4]. «la cathédrale tout entière n’est qu’une glorification muette, mais imagée, de l’antique science d’Hermès» (the whole cathedral is nothing but a mute but pictured glorification of the ancient science of Hermes) [PARIS.1 ¶12]. The paradox is named: «le flambeau de l’alchimique pensée illuminant le temple de la pensée chrétienne !» (the torch of alchemical thought lighting the temple of Christian thought!) [PARIS.5 ¶20].

Stated. The two levels are themselves carved: the figure of alchemy holds «deux livres, l’un fermé (ésotérisme), l’autre ouvert (exotérisme)» (two books, one closed (esotericism), the other open (exotericism)) [PARIS.1 ¶3]. The open level is secular and popular: «Rien de plus laïque que l’exotérisme de cet enseignement !» (Nothing more secular than the exotericism of this teaching!) [INTRO.2 ¶4]. The method aims at the closed book (interpreted from PARIS.1 ¶3 with PARIS.7 ¶35, where the uninstructed visitor finds only «l’exotérisme légendaire de saint Marcel», the legendary exotericism of saint Marcel [PARIS.7 ¶35]).

2. The key: art gothique is argot, a spoken cabala

Stated. The key to the stone book is a reading of its own name. The word gothique is not to be explained by Goths or by “barbarous”: «l’explication doit en être recherchée dans l’origine cabalistique du mot plutôt que dans sa racine littérale» (the explanation must be sought in the cabalistic origin of the word rather than in its literal root) [INTRO.3 ¶3]. The people kept the right name against the Academy’s Art ogival: «La vérité, qui sort de la bouche du peuple, a pourtant maintenu et conservé l’expression d’Art gothique» (Truth, which comes from the mouth of the people, has nevertheless maintained and preserved the expression Gothic Art) [INTRO.3 ¶3]. Some authors noted «similitude qui existe entre gothique et goétique» (the similarity between gothic and goetic) [INTRO.3 ¶4]; Fulcanelli’s own reading is different.

Stated. «art gothique n’est qu’une déformation orthographique du mot argotique, dont l’homophonie est parfaite» (gothic art is only an orthographic deformation of the word argotique, with which it is perfectly homophonous), by «la loi phonétique qui régit, dans toutes les langues et sans tenir compte de l’orthographe, la cabale traditionnelle» (the phonetic law which governs, in all languages and without regard to spelling, the traditional cabala) [INTRO.3 ¶5]. Hence «La cathédrale est une œuvre d’art goth ou d’argot» (The cathedral is a work of goth art or of argot) [INTRO.3 ¶5]. The dictionaries (QUOTED: les dictionnaires, adopted) define argot as «un langage particulier à tous les individus qui ont intérêt à se communiquer leurs pensées sans être compris de ceux qui les entourent» (a language particular to all individuals who have an interest in communicating their thoughts without being understood by those around them); so «C’est donc bien une cabale parlée» (It is therefore indeed a spoken cabala) [INTRO.3 ¶5].

Stated. The speakers of argot are given a lineage by sound: the argotiers are «descendants hermétiques des argo-nautes, lesquels montaient le navire Argo, parlaient la langue argotique, — notre langue verte» (hermetic descendants of the argo-nauts, who manned the ship Argo, spoke the argotic tongue — our green tongue) on their way to the Golden Fleece [INTRO.3 ¶5]. «Tous les Initiés s’exprimaient en argot» (All Initiates expressed themselves in argot): the truands of the Cour des Miracles with Villon, and the «Frimasons, ou francs-maçons du moyen âge, “logeurs du bon Dieu”» (Frimasons, or freemasons of the Middle Ages, “lodgers of the good God”), who built the works [INTRO.3 ¶5]. Today’s outlaws who speak it are called «voyous, c’est-à-dire de voyants» (voyous, that is to say seers), and the closing reading is «L’art gothique est, en effet, l’art got ou cot (Χο), l’art de la Lumière ou de l’Esprit» (Gothic art is, in fact, the art got or cot (Χο), the art of Light or of the Spirit) [INTRO.3 ¶6]. The text gives no further derivation of cot than the Greek letters in parentheses.

Stated. Fulcanelli concedes the obvious objection and answers it with a doctrine of language: «Ce sont là, pensera-t-on, de simples jeux de mots. Nous en convenons volontiers.» (These are, one will think, mere plays on words. We willingly agree.) «L’essentiel est qu’ils guident notre foi vers une certitude, vers la vérité positive et scientifique, clef du mystère religieux» (The essential thing is that they guide our faith towards a certainty, towards positive and scientific truth, key of the religious mystery) [INTRO.3 ¶7]. The justification is that nothing in language is accidental: «Il n’y a, ici-bas, ni hasard, ni coïncidence, ni rapport fortuit ; tout est prévu, ordonné, réglé» (There is, here below, neither chance, nor coincidence, nor fortuitous relation; all is foreseen, ordered, ruled) [INTRO.3 ¶7]. Words have a shell and a spirit: «si nous savions disséquer les mots, en briser l’écorce et libérer l’esprit, divine lumière qu’ils renferment» (if we knew how to dissect words, break their bark and free the spirit, divine light, that they enclose) [INTRO.3 ¶7]. Parables and puns are of the same kind: «Jésus ne s’exprimait qu’en paraboles» (Jesus expressed himself only in parables), and the witty are «cabalistes sans le savoir» (cabalists without knowing it) [INTRO.3 ¶7].

Stated. Argot is one derived form of an older tongue: «l’argot est une des formes dérivées de la Langue des Oiseaux, mère et doyenne de toutes les autres, la langue des philosophes et des diplomates» (argot is one of the derived forms of the Language of the Birds, mother and eldest of all the others, the language of philosophers and of diplomats) [INTRO.3 ¶8]. The ancient Incas called it «Langue de cour, parce qu’elle était familière aux diplomates» (Court language, because it was familiar to the diplomats), to whom it gave «la clef d’une double science : la science sacrée et la science profane» (the key of a double science: the sacred science and the profane science); the Middle Ages called it «Gaie science ou Gay sçavoir, Langue des dieux, Dive-Bouteille» (Gay science or Gay knowing, Language of the gods, Divine Bottle); «C’est elle dont Jésus révèle la connaissance à ses apôtres, en leur envoyant son esprit, l’Esprit-Saint» (It is this whose knowledge Jesus reveals to his apostles by sending them his spirit, the Holy Spirit); men spoke it before Babel, and its traces survive in argot, Picard, Provençal and the dialect of the gypsies [INTRO.3 ¶8]. Rabelais’s Gargantua et Pantagruel is «une œuvre ésotérique, un roman d’argot» (an esoteric work, a novel in argot) [INTRO.3 n1]. Tirésias, said to have known this language, shared it with Thalès, Melampus and Apollonios de Tyane, «personnages fictifs dont les noms parlent éloquemment» (fictitious figures whose names speak eloquently) [INTRO.3 ¶9]; only Apollonios is analysed, his name seeming «une image mytho-hermétique du compost, ou rebis philosophal» (a mytho-hermetic image of the compost, or philosophic rebis) [INTRO.3 n4].

Stated. The author’s own practice is covered by the same word: he cites Artephius (QUOTED: Artephius, adopted), «Notre Art, disait jadis Artephius, est entièrement cabalistique» (Our Art, Artephius once said, is entirely cabalistic), and says the cabala let him, «sans truquer la vérité, sans dénaturer l’expression, sans falsifier la Science ni nous parjurer, de dire plusieurs choses qu’on chercherait vainement dans les livres de nos prédécesseurs» (without faking the truth, without distorting expression, without falsifying the Science or perjuring ourselves, say several things one would seek in vain in the books of our predecessors) [AMIENS ¶21]. The Philosophers’ books are «fermés, comme celui de l’Apocalypse, par des sceaux cabalistiques. Il vous faut les briser un à un.» (closed, like that of the Apocalypse, by cabalistic seals. You must break them one by one.) [PARIS.3 ¶34].

Stated. This cabala is set against another: the reading of the oak «ne saurait parler mieux sans outrepasser certaines bornes» (could not be put better without overstepping certain bounds), and «Seuls, les initiés au langage des dieux comprendront sans aucune peine, parce qu’ils possèdent les clefs qui ouvrent toutes les portes» (Only the initiates in the language of the gods will understand without any trouble, because they hold the keys that open all doors); the «docteurs en kabbale» (doctors in kabbalah), Jewish or Christian, are dismissed, their «combinaisons illusoires ne conduisent à rien de solide, de positif ni de scientifique» (illusory combinations lead to nothing solid, positive or scientific) [BOURGES.2 ¶21]. The text does not say here what distinguishes the two in method; Canseliet does (section 10).

What is not said. The “phonetic law” is never stated as a set of rules. The one place that names rules, the Hendaye inscription, refers the reader to Grasset d’Orcet’s analysis of the Songe de Polyphile for the rules of the Diplomatique and says they were given clearly enough to spare him repeating them [HENDAYE ¶9]. The procedure there is described, not codified: read the Latin as written but in French, the «langue des diplomates»; apply the permuting vowels; take the assonance of the new words; restore spelling, word order and literary sense [HENDAYE ¶9]. What follows is therefore a collection of worked cases, not a grammar (interpreted).

3. Worked readings by sound and root

Each is recorded exactly as far as the text takes it. Nothing is extended.
