Gothic churches are mostly laid out as a Latin cross, and ‘the cross is the alchemical hieroglyph of the crucible’ [INTRO.4 ¶1]. Its three nails image the three purifications by iron and fire [INTRO.4 ¶3]. It is not specifically Christian [INTRO.4 ¶4]. Through the sign of the Cross the plan reveals the qualities and preparation of the first matter, leading to the first stone [INTRO.4 ¶5]. On the tympanum of the north portal of Notre-Dame, as in spagyric notation, the four crosses represent the imperfect metals [PARIS.6 ¶1, PARIS.6 ¶2, PARIS.6 ¶9, PARIS.6 ¶10]. At Hendaye, the upper cross is the divine one, the lower the cross of the globe locating the northern pole; INRI is read Igne Natura Renovatur Integra [HENDAYE ¶10].

«Or, la croix est l’hiéroglyphe alchimique du creuset» (Now, the cross is the alchemical hieroglyph of the crucible) [INTRO.4 ¶1]

«Souvenez-vous aussi, alchimistes mes frères, que la croix porte l’empreinte des trois clous qui servirent à immoler le Christ-matière, image des trois purifications par le fer et par le feu.» (Remember too, alchemists my brothers, that the cross bears the imprint of the three nails used to sacrifice the Christ-matter, image of the three purifications by iron and by fire.) [INTRO.4 ¶3]

Cross of Hendaye — croix d’Hendaye

place; Name as given in the text.

A plain stone cross by the south transept of Hendaye’s parish church, moved from the cemetery in 1842; origin unknown, dated by Fulcanelli not before the late 17th or early 18th century [HENDAYE ¶2]. He calls it the most singular monument of primitive millenarianism; its rough reliefs show deep science [HENDAYE ¶2–3]. On the Greek cross: OCRUXAVES / PESUNICA, read as a deliberate sign [HENDAYE ¶4–9]; on the front INRI [HENDAYE ¶10]. The pedestal bears the sun, the moon, a large star and a circle quartered by two diameters with an A in each sector, the four ages of the world [HENDAYE ¶11].

«Quoiqu’il en soit de son ancienneté, la croix d’Hendaye, par la décoration de son piédestal, se montre bien le plus singulier monument du millénarisme primitif, la plus rare traduction symbolique du chiliasme, que nous ayons jamais rencontrés.» (Whatever its age, the cross of Hendaye, by the decoration of its pedestal, is indeed the most singular monument of primitive millenarianism, the rarest symbolic rendering of chiliasm, that we have ever met.) [HENDAYE ¶2]

Crucible — creuset

term; Literal translation; the text gives old forms cruzol, crucible, croiset and the root crux via crucibulum [INTRO.4 ¶1].

The cross is its hieroglyph; low-Latin crucibulum has the root crux, cross [INTRO.4 ¶1]. In it the first matter suffers the Passion, dies and rises purified [INTRO.4 ¶2]. The dry way cooks the Celestial Salt with a metallic body in a crucible on a naked fire [PARIS.7 ¶4]; the Regime of Saturn needs only a crucible instead of glass [PARIS.7 ¶7]; Elias showed the stone stuck to crucible shards [PARIS.7 ¶10]. A relief shows a child falling from a crucible held by an archangel, the Massacre of the Innocents [PARIS.6 ¶22].

«C’est en effet dans le creuset que la matière première, comme le Christ lui-même, souffre la Passion ; c’est dans le creuset qu’elle meurt pour ressusciter ensuite, purifiée, spiritualisée, déjà transformée.» (It is indeed in the crucible that the first matter, like Christ himself, suffers the Passion; it is in the crucible that it dies, to rise again afterwards, purified, spiritualized, already transformed.) [INTRO.4 ¶2]

«La cuisson de l’Œuvre, au lieu de nécessiter l’emploi d’un vase de verre, ne réclame que le secours d’un simple creuset.» (The cooking of the Work, instead of requiring a glass vessel, calls only for a simple crucible.) [PARIS.7 ¶7]

Crypt — crypte

term; The usual English term; the text glosses it from the Greek for ‘hidden’ [INTRO.8 ¶1].

The set of hidden passages beneath the church floor, named from the Greek for ‘hidden’ [INTRO.8 ¶1]. A low, damp, cold place giving the sense of power joined to darkness, the dwelling of the dead [INTRO.8 ¶2]; its massive pillars express a real but occult force working in secret [INTRO.8 ¶3]. Formerly the underground chambers of temples housed statues of Isis, which became the Black Virgins; since these figure the black primitive earth, the subject of the Work, it is fitting that they live in the underground places of temples [INTRO.8 ¶4]. Chartres’s Notre-Dame-sous-Terre sits in the crypt [INTRO.8 ¶5].

«Leur ensemble, qui s’étend sous le sol de l’église, constitue la crypte (du grec Κρυπτος, caché).» (Taken together, they extend beneath the floor of the church and make up the crypt (from the Greek kryptos, hidden).) [INTRO.8 ¶1]

«Force réelle, mais occulte, qui s’exerce dans le secret, se développe dans l’ombre, agit sans trêve dans la profondeur des substructions de l’œuvre.» (A real but occult force, which works in secret, develops in the shadow, and acts unceasingly in the depth of the work’s substructures.) [INTRO.8 ¶3]

Crédayougam — Crédayougam

term; Name as spelled in the text.

In Hindu myth as given by the text, the first age, the age of innocence, answering to the Greeks’ golden age: the cow of Virtue stands firmly on all four feet [HENDAYE ¶14].

«Au premier âge, qui répond à l’âge d’or des Grecs et que l’on nomme Crédayougam ou âge d’innocence, la Vertu se tient ferme sur la terre : la vache pose en plein sur ses quatre pieds.» (In the first age, which answers to the golden age of the Greeks and is called Crédayougam or age of innocence, Virtue stands firm on the earth: the cow rests fully on its four feet.) [HENDAYE ¶14]

Cum luce salutem — With the light, salvation — Cum luce salutem

work; Latin kept; the English follows the book’s own French gloss ‘avec la lumière, le salut’ [PREF2 ¶24].

Latin legend of a painting at the Franciscan convent of Cimiez showing the two stars; Canseliet says it expresses the saving virtue of nocturnal starlight [PREF2 ¶23, PREF2 ¶24].

«« Cum luce salutem ; avec la lumière, le salut. »» (‘Cum luce salutem; with the light, salvation.’ (French gloss is the book’s.)) [PREF2 ¶24]

Curiositez de Paris — Curiosities of Paris — Curiositez de Paris

work; Name as spelled in the text.

Published in 1716 by Saugrain the elder; cited for the schools of the Angelic Doctor beside the Jacobins’ church [PARIS.8 ¶4].

«Les Curiositez de Paris, parues en 1716 chez Saugrain l’aîné» (The Curiosities of Paris, published in 1716 by Saugrain the elder) [PARIS.8 ¶4]

Cybele — Cybèle

person; Name as spelled in the text.

The epithet ‘mother of the gods’ applied to Isis also applied to Rhea or Cybele; Fulcanelli inclines to see them as different expressions of one principle [INTRO.8 ¶15]. Charles Vincens describes a bas-relief of Cybele at Pennes inscribed Matri Deum [INTRO.8 ¶15]. Cybele was worshipped at Pessinus in Phrygia as a black stone said to have fallen from the sky, which the text calls a singular hermetic analogy; Phidias showed her enthroned between two lions with a mural crown and a veil; sometimes she holds a key and seems to draw aside her veil [INTRO.8 ¶15]. The sceptre with which Rhea strikes Mount Dindymus is one of several images of the hidden matter of the Philosophers [INTRO.9 ¶16].

«Cybèle était adorée à Pessinonte, en Phrygie, sous la forme d’une pierre noire que l’on disait être tombée du ciel.» (Cybele was worshipped at Pessinus, in Phrygia, in the form of a black stone said to have fallen from the sky.) [INTRO.8 ¶15]

Identified in the text with: Isis

Cyliani — Cyliani

person; Name as spelled in the text.

Author cited from the Preface of his book (Hermès dévoilé, Paris 1832) [PARIS.7 ¶8, PARIS.7 n5]: two matters of one origin, one volatile, one fixed; two ways, dry and humid; he follows the humid ‘by duty’, though the dry, made with a single matter, is familiar to him [PARIS.7 ¶9]. He described the combat of the alchemist and the dragon [BOURGES.1 ¶9]. Canseliet: Cyliani opened the door of the labyrinth to him in 1915 [PREF2 ¶4]; meditating the Adepts’ words on the two stars gives ‘the key with which Cyliani opens the lock of the temple’ [PREF2 ¶25].

«Je suis cette dernière, de préférence, par devoir, quoique la première me soit très familière : elle se fait avec une matière unique.» (I follow the latter by preference, out of duty, though the former is very familiar to me: it is done with a single matter.) [PARIS.7 ¶9]

Cyprian Piccolpassi — Piccolpassi

person; Name as spelled in the text (‘cavalier Cyprian Piccolpassi’, BOURGES.2 n14).

For making the vessel, the potter’s craft is more instructive than glassmaking; among Piccolpassi’s plates is a dove whose feet are tied to a stone [BOURGES.2 ¶36]. The footnote cites ‘Claudius Popelin, Les Trois Livres de l’Art du Potier, du cavalier Cyprian Piccolpassi’ (Paris, Librairie Internationale, 1861) [BOURGES.2 n14].

«voyez les planches de Piccolpassi, vous en trouverez une qui représente une colombe dont les pattes sont attachées à une pierre.» (look at Piccolpassi’s plates; you will find one showing a dove whose feet are tied to a stone.) [BOURGES.2 ¶36]

Cyrano Bergerac — Cyrano Bergerac

person; Name as spelled in the text (‘de Cyrano Bergerac’).

Called ‘the great philosopher’; his description of the duel of the Remora and the Salamander is, for Fulcanelli, the best and most initiatic account of the violent reaction in the Conjunction [PARIS.5 ¶12, BOURGES.1 ¶9]. Canseliet cites him for calling the matrix language ‘the instinct or voice of Nature’ [PREF2 ¶45].

«Mais, à notre avis, la meilleure description, — la plus initiatique surtout, — est celle que nous laissa le grand philosophe de Cyrano Bergerac du duel effroyable que se livrèrent, sous ses yeux, la Rémore et la Salamandre.» (But in our opinion the best description, — the most initiatic above all, — is the one the great philosopher de Cyrano Bergerac left us of the dreadful duel fought before his eyes by the Remora and the Salamander.) [PARIS.5 ¶12]

D

Dampierre-sur-Boutonne — Dampierre-sur-Boutonne

place; Name as spelled in the text.

Site of one of two soffits with initiatic subjects comparable to the Lallemant chapel ceiling: sculpted, 16th century, with a reference to Les Demeures Philosophales [BOURGES.2 n12].

«Deux inestimables soffites, à sujets initiatiques, peuvent lui être comparés : l’un, à Dampierre-sur-Boutonne, également sculpté, du XVIe siècle (Les Demeures Philosophales)» (Two priceless soffits with initiatic subjects can be compared with it: one at Dampierre-sur-Boutonne, likewise sculpted, of the 16th century (Les Demeures Philosophales)) [BOURGES.2 n12]

David de Planis-Campy — David de Planis-Campy

person; Name as spelled in the text.

«David de Planis-Campy le cite, en effet, comme possédant « le don précieux de la pierre au blanc », en d’autres termes de la transmutation des métaux vils en argent.» (David de Planis-Campy indeed cites him as possessing ‘the precious gift of the stone to the white’, in other words of the transmutation of base metals into silver.) [BOURGES.1 ¶3]

De dictis Germanicis — On German Sayings — De dictis Germanicis

de Laborde — de Laborde

person; Name as spelled in the text.

Called ‘the sagacious de Laborde’, whose attention the hermetic emblem of the Saint Anne porch had already drawn in the seventeenth century [PARIS.7 ¶11]. His work is Explications de l’Enigme trouvée à un pilier de l’Eglise Notre-Dame de Paris, Paris, 1636 [PARIS.7 n8].

«L’emblème hermétique de Notre-Dame, qui avait déjà, au XVIIè siècle, fixé l’attention du sagace de Laborde» (The hermetic emblem of Notre-Dame, which had already, in the seventeenth century, held the attention of the sagacious de Laborde) [PARIS.7 ¶11]

De Nuysement — De Nuysement

person; Name as spelled in the text.

Author of the Poème philosophic de la Vérité de la Phisique Minéralle, in the Traitez de l’Harmonie et Constitution generalle du Vray Sel (Paris, 1620 and 1621, p. 254) [INTRO.6 n1]. The verses on the wheel and the even heat that the artist must keep, neither too high nor too low, are cited from him [INTRO.6 ¶4–11; INTRO.6 n1].

«Remarque seulement les traces de ma roue.» (Only mark the tracks of my wheel.) [INTRO.6 ¶5]

Denys Zachaire — Denys Zachaire

person; Name as spelled in the text.

He tells that the alchemists’ weekly meetings at Notre-Dame de Paris still went on in 1539, on Sundays and feast days [INTRO.2 ¶5]. Named with ‘messire Bernard, comte de Trévise’ and Flamel as of a time now past [INTRO.9 ¶2].

«Denys Zachaire nous apprend que l’usage s’y maintenait encore en l’an 1539, « les dimanches et jours de festes »» (Denys Zachaire tells us that the custom still held there in the year 1539, ‘on Sundays and feast days’) [INTRO.2 ¶5]

Des choses qui arrivèrent en Perse — Of the Things that Happened in Persia at the Birth of Christ — Des choses qui arrivèrent en Perse, lors de la naissance du Christ

work; Title as spelled in the text, with our English rendering.

Legend attributed to Julius Africanus, of uncertain date [INTRO.7 ¶12]. In a temple of Juno built by Cyrus, a priest announces that Juno has conceived; the statues dance; a star descends and announces the birth of a Child Principle and End; the statues fall face down; the Magi announce the Child born at Bethlehem; Bacchus foretells that the Child will drive out the false gods; the Magi, guided by the star, salute Mary, have her portrait with the Child painted and dedicate it ‘to Jupiter Mithra’ (glossed ‘to the sun god’) and to King Jesus [INTRO.7 ¶13].

«Une étoile descend et annonce la naissance d’un Enfant Principe et Fin.» (A star descends and announces the birth of a Child who is Beginning and End.) [INTRO.7 ¶13]

«Frédéric Portal, Des Couleurs Symboliques. Paris, Treuttel et Würtz, 1857, p. 2.» (Frédéric Portal, On Symbolic Colours. Paris, Treuttel et Würtz, 1857, p. 2.) [PARIS.3 n12]

Deux Logis alchimiques — Two Alchemical Dwellings — Deux Logis alchimiques

work; Name as spelled in the text; English title is our literal translation.

Work in which Canseliet (‘we’) wrote on the centaur, the man-horse of Plessis-Bourré [PREF2 ¶45]; a note cites it for the painted soffit of Plessis-Bourré [BOURGES.2 n12].

«l’autre, au Plessis-Bourré, composé de peintures, du XVe siècle (Deux Logis Alchimiques).» (the other, at Plessis-Bourré, made of paintings, of the 15th century (Two Alchemical Dwellings).) [BOURGES.2 n12]

«après les brèves considérations que nous avons apportées à l’occasion du centaure, de l’homme-cheval du Plessis-Bourré, dans Deux Logis alchimiques» (after the brief remarks we offered on the centaur, the man-horse of Plessis-Bourré, in Two Alchemical Dwellings) [PREF2 ¶45]

work; Name as spelled in the text.

Dictionnaire Mytho-Hermétique — Mytho-Hermetic Dictionary — Dictionnaire Mytho-Hermétique

work; Title as spelled in the text, with our literal English.

Pernety’s dictionary, noted for not citing the term ‘Miroir de l’Art’ [PARIS.5 ¶4].

«Pernety, dans son Dictionnaire Mytho-Hermétique, n’a point cité ce terme, soit qu’il ne l’ait pas connu, soit qu’il l’ait volontairement omis.» (Pernety, in his Mytho-Hermetic Dictionary, did not cite this term, either because he did not know it or because he left it out on purpose.) [PARIS.5 ¶4]

Dijon Infantry — Infanterie dijonnaise

term; Literal translation.

Processions and banquets called the last echo of the Feast of Fools, with their Mère Folle (Mother Fool), Rabelaisian diplomas and a banner with two brothers head to tail showing their buttocks [INTRO.1 ¶15].

«les processions et banquets de l’Infanterie dijonnaise, dernier écho de la Fête des Fous, avec sa Mère Folle, ses diplômes rabelaisiens, son guidon où deux frères, tête-bêche, se plaisent à découvrir leurs fesses» (the processions and banquets of the Dijon Infantry, last echo of the Feast of Fools, with its Mother Fool, its Rabelaisian diplomas, its banner on which two brothers, head to tail, delight in baring their buttocks) [INTRO.1 ¶15]

Diodorus of Tarsus — Diodore de Tarse

person; Name as spelled in the text.

Quoted as more positive still than Chalcidius: the star of the Nativity was not one of the stars of the sky but a certain virtue or force that took the form of a star to announce the birth of the Lord of all [INTRO.7 ¶18]. Source: Du Destin, in Photius, cod. 233 [INTRO.7 n9].

«cette étoile n’était pas une de celles qui peuplent le ciel, mais une certaine vertu ou force (δυναμις) urano-diurne (θειοτεραν), ayant pris la forme d’un astre pour annoncer la naissance du Seigneur de tous» (this star was not one of those that people the sky, but a certain virtue or force (dynamis), urano-diurnal (theioteran), which had taken the form of a star to announce the birth of the Lord of all) [INTRO.7 ¶18]

Diplomatics — Diplomatique

term; Literal translation; the text links diplomates to the Language of the Birds [INTRO.3 ¶8].

The Language of the Birds is the language of philosophers and diplomates; the ancient Incas called it Language of the court because it gave diplomates the key to a double science, sacred and profane [INTRO.3 ¶8]. At Hendaye the inscription must be read in the language of the gods or birds by the rules of the Diplomatique: read the Latin as written in French, ‘langue des diplomates’, permuting vowels [HENDAYE ¶9].

«Les anciens Incas l’appelaient Langue de cour, parce qu’elle était familière aux diplomates, à qui elle donnait la clef d’une double science : la science sacrée et la science profane.» (The ancient Incas called it the Language of the court, because it was familiar to the diplomats, to whom it gave the key to a double science: sacred science and profane science.) [INTRO.3 ¶8]

«et qu’il faut en découvrir le sens à l’aide des règles de la Diplomatique.» (and that its meaning must be discovered with the help of the rules of Diplomatics.) [HENDAYE ¶9]

Discours philosophique sur les Trois Principes — Philosophical Discourse on the Three Principles — Discours philosophique sur les Trois Principes

work; Name as spelled in the text.

Work of Sabine Stuart de Chevalier, also titled Clef du Sanctuaire philosophique, Paris, Quillau, 1781 [PARIS.5 n4]; the source of the passage on dissolving common mercury that Fulcanelli rejects [PARIS.5 ¶7].

«Sabine Stuart de Chevalier, Discours philosophique sur les Trois Principes, ou la Clef du Sanctuaire philosophique. Paris, Quillau, 1781.» (Sabine Stuart de Chevalier, Philosophical Discourse on the Three Principles, or the Key of the Philosophical Sanctuary. Paris, Quillau, 1781.) [PARIS.5 n4]

Dispute avec Tryphon — Dialogue with Trypho — Dispute avec Tryphon

work; Title as given in the text (Latin Dialogus cum Tryphone), with the usual English rendering.

Work the text attributes to Saint Augustine (ch. 40), quoted on the lamb, its roasting in the form of a cross, and the believers who dye their dwellings, that is themselves, by faith [INTRO.4 ¶3].

«dans sa Dispute avec Tryphon (Dialogus cum Tryphone, 40)» (in his Dispute with Trypho (Dialogus cum Tryphone, 40)) [INTRO.4 ¶3]

Dissolution — Dissolution

term; The usual English term for it.

In the philosophical Dissolution the volatile parts extract the fixed, central and pure parts; the horseman thrown by his horse on the first lower medallion of Paris stands for it [PARIS.5 ¶1, PARIS.5 ¶2]. The lower medallions as a group show the points that vary in the practice of the Solution, which alone identifies the path followed: dissolving alchemical gold with the Alkahest marks the first path, dissolving common gold with ‘our mercury’ the second [PARIS.5 ¶14]. A second solution, that of the red or white Sulphur by the philosophical water, is the subject of the twelfth medallion [PARIS.5 ¶15]. The reign of Saturn is the emblem of radical dissolution, decomposition and the black colour [PARIS.5 ¶9]. In the Conjunction, the dissolution of the gold is gentle in one path and violent, like an effervescence, in the other [PARIS.5 ¶12]. A footnote gives the name Key to any radical alchemical dissolution [PARIS.3 n6].

«La dissolution de l’or alchimique par le Dissolvant Alkaest caractérise la première voie ; celle de l’or vulgaire par notre mercure indique la seconde.» (The dissolution of alchemical gold by the Alkahest Solvent characterises the first path; that of common gold by our mercury indicates the second.) [PARIS.5 ¶14]

Dog of Corascene — chien de Corascène

term; Literal translation.

The dog carved on the base of the Portal of the Virgin belongs to the animation of the exalted mercury; it is the dog of Corascene spoken of by Arthépius and Philalethes, which must be separated from the compost in the state of black powder [PARIS.6 ¶11; PARIS.6 ¶12].

«ce chien de Corascène, dont parlent Arthépius et Philalèthe, qu’il faut savoir séparer du compost à l’état de poudre noire» (this dog of Corascene, of which Arthepius and Philalethes speak, which one must know how to separate from the compost in the state of black powder) [PARIS.6 ¶12]

Dolphin (Dauphin) — Dauphin

term; Literal translation; French Dauphin is both the fish and the heir’s title, as the text notes (BOURGES.2 ¶15).

The dolphin was a ‘royal fish’; the name was symbolic, since the kings’ eldest son bore the title Dauphin [BOURGES.2 ¶15]. The fishermen of the Mutus Liber try to net a dolphin; dolphins appear on the Hôtel Lallemant (corner turret window, a pillar capital, the credence) [BOURGES.2 ¶15]. On the chapel capital a vase flanked by two dolphins stands for the dissolvent or common mercury of the Philosophers [BOURGES.2 ¶26]; two symmetrical dolphins, tied at their ends along the axis, crown the credence [BOURGES.2 ¶30].

«puisque le fils aîné des rois, celui qui devait ceindre la couronne, portait toujours le titre de Dauphin, nom d’un poisson, et, qui mieux est, d’un poisson royal.» (since the kings’ eldest son, the one who was to wear the crown, always bore the title of Dauphin, the name of a fish and, better still, of a royal fish.) [BOURGES.2 ¶15]

«C’est, d’ailleurs, un dauphin que les pêcheurs en barque du Mutus Liber cherchent à capturer au filet et à l’hameçon.» (It is, moreover, a dolphin that the fishermen in a boat in the Mutus Liber try to catch with net and hook.) [BOURGES.2 ¶15]

Douze Clefs de la Philosophie — The Twelve Keys of Philosophy — Douze Clefs de la Philosophie

work; Name as spelled in the text; English title is our literal translation.

Work of Brother Basile Valentin (Paris, Moët, 1659; reissued 1956) [PARIS.4 n4]. Its third key uses the cock-and-fox figure for the extraction of the red sulphur [PARIS.4 ¶19]; one figure of the ‘Livre des Douze Clefs’ shows the vessel with smoke rising to a window showing sun and moon, the origin of the mercurial water [PARIS.4 ¶14]. Fulcanelli warns against taking literally its saying that whoever has the matter will find a pot to cook it [BOURGES.2 ¶34]. Canseliet wrote an Introduction to it [PREF2 ¶5] and quotes its two stars given to man by the Gods [PREF2 ¶22].

«Ne vous fiez pas à ce que dit Basile Valentin dans ses Douze Clefs, et gardez-vous de prendre ses paroles à la lettre lorsqu’il prétend que « celui qui a la matière trouvera bien un pot pour la cuire ».» (Do not trust what Basil Valentine says in his Twelve Keys, and beware of taking his words literally when he claims that ‘whoever has the matter will surely find a pot to cook it in’.) [BOURGES.2 ¶34]

Douze Portes — Twelve Gates — Douze Portes

work; Name as spelled in the text.

Work of ‘Ripleus Anglois’, cited inside a quotation from the commentary on La Lumière sortant des Ténèbres: at its beginning Ripley says the same as that author about the essence in which the sought spirit lives [AMIENS ¶28]. The ‘first gate of Ripley’ is named as the first step of practice [PARIS.5 ¶11].

«la même chose est dite par Ripleus Anglois au commencement de ses Douze Portes» (the same thing is said by Ripley the Englishman at the beginning of his Twelve Gates) [AMIENS ¶28]

Doves of Diana — Colombes de Diane

term; Literal translation.

The two doves on the base of the Portal of the Virgin, described in the animation of the exalted mercury, are the Doves of Diana, a ‘despairing enigma’ under which the spiritualisation and sublimation of the philosophical mercury are hidden [PARIS.6 ¶11; PARIS.6 ¶12].

«ces Colombes de Diane, autre énigme désespérante, sous laquelle la spiritualisation et sublimation du mercure philosophal sont cachées.» (these Doves of Diana, another despairing enigma, under which the spiritualisation and sublimation of the philosophical mercury are hidden.) [PARIS.6 ¶12]

Dr Evans — docteur Evans

person; Name as spelled in the text.

Of Oxford; discovered in 1902 the labyrinth of Cnossos in Crete, which was called Absolum [INTRO.5 ¶7].

«celui de Cnossos en Crète, qui fut découvert en 1902 par le docteur Evans, d’Oxford, était appelé Absolum.» (that of Cnossos in Crete, which was discovered in 1902 by Dr Evans of Oxford, was called Absolum.) [INTRO.5 ¶7]

Dragon — dragon

term; Literal translation.

In the motif of the Saint-Anne porch [PARIS.7 ¶1], after the pure principles rise, the residue yields the volatile, fusible mercurial salt that old authors call the ‘Dragon babylonien’ [PARIS.7 ¶20]; the sculpted dragon emerges from the flames and clasps the athanor [PARIS.7 ¶21]. The dragon is the image of mercury, on which the nourishing of the Sulphur or Elixir should be sought [PARIS.7 ¶23]; covered with solar circles, it can transmute in quantity, but only after further cookings with the Sulphur, the multiplications [PARIS.7 ¶27]. Basil Valentine teaches the making of the Green Lion under the fight of eagle and dragon; this combat of chemical bodies yields the secret dissolvent and recurs in Cadmos, Jason, Saint Michael, Saint George, Saint Marcel [BOURGES.1 ¶9]. Whoever knows the dragon knows how to defeat it [BOURGES.2 ¶20]. In the third preface Canseliet sees in Saint Marcel’s dragon legend the two ways, dry and wet [PREF3 ¶42, ¶43].

«C’est donc sur le dragon, image du mercure, que nous devrions chercher le symbole représentatif de la nutrition et de la progression du Soufre ou de l’Elixir.» (It is therefore on the dragon, image of mercury, that we should look for the symbol representing the nourishment and progression of the Sulphur or Elixir.) [PARIS.7 ¶23]

«le résidu est prêt, dès lors, à fournir le sel mercuriel, volatil et fusible, auquel les vieux auteurs ont souvent donné l’épithète de Dragon babylonien.» (the residue is then ready to yield the mercurial salt, volatile and fusible, to which the old authors often gave the epithet Babylonian Dragon.) [PARIS.7 ¶20]

Dry water — eau sèche

term; Literal translation.

From the combination of the primitive water from the Chaos of the Sages with a second water comes a third water ‘that does not wet the hands’, called Mercury or Sulphur according to its quality or aspect [PARIS.2 ¶7]. Limojon de Saint-Didier, citing the Cosmopolite, says the secret fire resolves the stone into dry water [PARIS.3 ¶22]. The servus fugitivus needed is a solid, brittle, stone-like mineral water, easily melted; this coagulated water is the Alkaest and Universal Dissolvent [PARIS.5 ¶7]. The water needed is dry, does not wet the hands and gushes under Aaron’s rod; though wholly volatile, it can be made fixed, the water becoming earth and the mercury sulphur [AMIENS ¶15].

«Car l’eau dont nous avons besoin est sèche, ne mouille pas les mains et jaillit sous le choc de la verge d’Aaron.» (For the water we need is dry, does not wet the hands, and gushes forth under the stroke of Aaron’s rod.) [AMIENS ¶15]

«C’est cette eau coagulée sous forme de masse pierreuse qui est l’Alkaest et le Dissolvant universel.» (It is this water, coagulated in the form of a stony mass, that is the Alkahest and the Universal Dissolvent.) [PARIS.5 ¶7]

Dry way — voie sèche

term; The usual English term; literal translation.

Of the two ways of the Work, the short and easy one; few alchemists admit there are two [PARIS.7 ¶3]. The south (Saint-Anne) porch of Notre-Dame describes the shortest practice [PARIS.7 ¶1]. Per the 18th-century Encyclopedia (QUOTED), it is less esteemed and consists in cooking the Celestial Salt, mercury of the Philosophers, with an earthly metallic body in a crucible on a naked fire for four days [PARIS.7 ¶4]; other authors give eight days or a week [PARIS.7 ¶5, ¶6]. The Sages named it the Regime of Saturn; it needs only a crucible instead of a glass vessel [PARIS.7 ¶7]. Cyliani (QUOTED): it is done with a single matter [PARIS.7 ¶8, ¶9]; Henckel imagines keeping the matters long in strongest fluidity by violent fire [PARIS.7 ¶10]. At the fourth degree of fire in the dry way about 1200° is required [AMIENS ¶12]. Canseliet finds both ways in the Saint Marcel legend [PREF3 ¶43].

«Peu d’alchimistes consentent à admettre la possibilité de deux voies, l’une courte et facile, nommée voie sèche, l’autre plus longue et plus ingrate, dite voie humide.» (Few alchemists consent to admit the possibility of two ways, one short and easy, called the dry way, the other longer and more thankless, called the wet way.) [PARIS.7 ¶3]

«Cette voie abrégée, mais couverte d’un voile épais, a été nommée par les Sages le Régime de Saturne.» (This shortened way, but covered with a thick veil, was named by the Sages the Regime of Saturn.) [PARIS.7 ¶7]

Du Cange — Du Cange

person; Name as spelled in the text (Ducange in INTRO.4; Glossarium Cangi in PREF3 n1).

Cited for the etymology that low-Latin crucibulum, crucible, has as root crux, crucis, cross [INTRO.4 ¶1]. His glossary gives orarium as the stole [PREF3 n1].

«dans la basse latinité, crucibulum, creuset, a pour racine crux, crucis, croix, d’après Ducange» (in low Latin, crucibulum, crucible, has as its root crux, crucis, cross, according to Ducange) [INTRO.4 ¶1]

Durandal — Durandal

term; Name as spelled in the text.

At Rocamadour, beside a giant Saint Christopher and an old iron-bound coffer, a rough sword fragment fixed in the rock by a chain is said by legend to belong to Durandal, the sword Roland broke opening the breach of Roncevaux [INTRO.9 ¶16]. The sword that opens the rock, Moses’s rod, Rhea’s sceptre and Atalanta’s javelin are one hieroglyph of the hidden matter; Saint Christopher shows its nature and the iron-bound coffer its result [INTRO.9 ¶16].

«sont un seul et même hiéroglyphe de cette matière cachée des Philosophes, dont saint Christophe indique la nature et le coffre ferré le résultat.» (are one and the same hieroglyph of that hidden matter of the Philosophers, whose nature Saint Christopher indicates and whose result the iron-bound coffer shows.) [INTRO.9 ¶16]

E

Eagle — Aigle

term; Literal translation.

The Sages figured the two natures by the Eagle and the Lion, equal in power but opposite in complexion: the eagle is the aerial, volatile force, the lion the earthly, fixed force [PARIS.4 ¶11]. They fight until the eagle loses its wings and the lion its head, making one body, the Animated Mercury [PARIS.4 ¶11]. The griffin has the head and breast of the eagle [PARIS.4 ¶3]. Eagle and lion are Nicolas Flamel’s figure for the combat of the conjunction [PARIS.5 ¶12]. Basile Valentin teaches the making of the Green Lion under the fight of the eagle and the dragon [BOURGES.1 ¶9]. In one legend the star of the Magi had the form of an eagle [INTRO.7 ¶11].

«Le lion traduit la force terrestre et fixe, tandis que l’aigle exprime la force aérienne et volatile.» (The lion expresses the earthly and fixed force, while the eagle expresses the aerial and volatile force.) [PARIS.4 ¶11]

Eagles — Aigles

term; Literal translation.

Stated: the philosophers’ name for the painful, tedious labour by which the first conjunction is achieved little by little; the reiteration of the Eagles is philosophical Sublimation [PARIS.4 ¶3]. To ‘make the eagle fly’ is to bring the light out of the tomb to the surface, the mark of every true sublimation [PARIS.4 ¶3]. Philalèthe (quoted): the exact preparation of the flying Eagles is the first degree of perfection; the Sages lead their eagles to devour the lion; at least seven eagles, even nine, are needed [PARIS.4 ¶3–4]. The seven circuits of Callimachus’s swans round Delos and Joshua’s seven rounds of Jericho are variants [PARIS.4 ¶5–10]. Identified with Sublimation (e345).

«Faire voler l’aigle, selon l’expression hermétique, c’est faire sortir la lumière du tombeau et la porter à la surface, ce qui est le propre de toute véritable sublimation.» (To make the eagle fly, in the hermetic expression, is to bring the light out of the tomb and carry it to the surface, which is the mark of every true sublimation.) [PARIS.4 ¶3]

«Mais pour perfectionner notre œuvre, il ne faut pas moins de sept aigles, et l’on devrait même en employer jusqu’à neuf.» (But to perfect our work, no fewer than seven eagles are needed, and one should even use as many as nine.) [PARIS.4 ¶4]

Identified in the text with: Sublimation

Echinéis — Echinéis

term; Name of the Cosmopolite’s fish as spelled in the text; the English keeps the name. The text’s own gloss: the sea urchin (BOURGES.2 ¶14).

Stated: the Cosmopolite’s famous fish, Echinéis, is the sea urchin (echinus), the bear cub, the Little Bear, the constellation of the pole star; hence Limojon de Saint-Didier tells seekers to steer ‘by the sight of the north star’ [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. The pole star is among the names of the Sign on the prepared mercury [BOURGES.2 ¶14].

«Ajoutons encore que le fameux poisson du Cosmopolite, qu’il appelle Echinéis, est l’oursin (echinus), l’ourson, la petite ourse, constellation dans laquelle se trouve l’étoile polaire.» (Let us add that the Cosmopolite’s famous fish, which he calls Echinéis, is the sea urchin (echinus), the bear cub, the Little Bear, the constellation in which the pole star lies.) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]

Elias Schadius — Elias Schadius

person; Name as spelled in the text.

Called ‘the learned’; in his book De dictis Germanicis he reported an inscription to Isis similar to Virgini parituræ, as cited by Pierre Dujols [INTRO.8 ¶4]. The footnote gives its French rendering [INTRO.8 n2].

«Isidi, seu Virgini ex qua filius proditurus est» (To Isis, or to the Virgin from whom the Son will be born (following the book’s French gloss)) [INTRO.8 ¶4]

Elias the artist — Elias

person; Name as spelled in the text.

Named in Henckel’s passage: the artist Elias, cited by Helvétius, claims the preparation of the stone begins and ends in four days and showed the stone still stuck to the crucible shards [PARIS.7 ¶10].

«l’artiste Elias, cité par Helvétius, prétend que la préparation de la pierre philosophale se commence et s’achève en quatre jours de temps» (the artist Elias, cited by Helvetius, claims that the preparation of the philosophers’ stone is begun and finished in four days’ time) [PARIS.7 ¶10]

Elijah — Elie

person; Name as spelled in the text; Elijah is the usual English form of the prophet’s name.

Arab authors, the text reports, teach that the waters of the fountain they call Holmat gave the prophet Elijah immortality; the text glosses his name with the Greek for sun (Ηλιος) [PARIS.2 ¶9]. In the reading of the Virgin as matter, the text writes ‘Elie, le Soleil Dieu le Père’ as the emblem of the vital spirit that animates the passive substance (Mary, the form) [PARIS.1 ¶8]. In the Hendaye chapter, the elect who will be saved in the cyclic catastrophe are called ‘enfants d’Elie’ [HENDAYE ¶9]; a list of initiatory orders includes ‘Chevaliers du Cygne, institués par Elie’ [PARIS.6 n4]. The text also names ‘l’artiste Elias, cité par Helvétius’ (quoting Henckel), who claimed the stone is begun and finished in four days and showed it still stuck to the crucible shards [PARIS.7 ¶10]; the text does not say whether this Elias is the prophet.

«ils nous enseignent, de plus que ses eaux donnèrent l’immortalité au prophète Elie (Ηλι ος, soleil).» (they teach us, moreover, that its waters gave immortality to the prophet Elijah (Helios, sun).) [PARIS.2 ¶9]

«Marie, Vierge et Mère, représente donc la forme ; Elie, le Soleil Dieu le Père est l’emblème de l’esprit vital.» (Mary, Virgin and Mother, therefore represents the form; Elijah, the Sun, God the Father, is the emblem of the vital spirit.) [PARIS.1 ¶8]

Elixation — élixation

term; The usual English term for it.

The text names elixation the wet phase of the linear coction, the first turn of the wheel, in which the compound stays molten until a light skin forms and thickens [AMIENS ¶7]. It is followed by assation [AMIENS ¶7].

«La première roue correspond à la phase humide de l’opération, — dénommée élixation, — où le composé demeure fondu, jusqu’à la formation d’une pellicule légère» (The first wheel corresponds to the wet phase of the operation, called elixation, in which the compound remains molten until a light film forms) [AMIENS ¶7]

Elixir — Elixir

term; The usual English term.

The Notre-Dame base lets one follow the work of the Elixir from first calcination to final coction [PARIS.2 ¶2]. The philosophic Mercury needs only graduated cooking to become red sulphur, then Elixir, then Universal Medicine [PARIS.3 ¶18]. Mercury carries nascent gold to the power of the Elixir [INTRO.9 ¶15]. The green lion is ‘the stone of our Elixir’ [PARIS.4 ¶17]; the despised subject becomes the Tree of Life, Elixir or Philosophers’ Stone [PARIS.5 ¶4]. The second of three identical operations gives the Elixir, medicine of the second order, differing from Sulphur in quality, not nature [BOURGES.2 ¶32]. Its multiplication needs the mercury [PARIS.7 ¶23]. The text thus uses Elixir both as a stage and, loosely, as a name of the finished stone.

«la seconde opération, absolument semblable à la première, fournit l’Elixir, ou médecine du second ordre, lequel n’est différent du Soufre qu’en qualité et non pas en nature» (the second operation, exactly like the first, yields the Elixir, or medicine of the second order, which differs from the Sulphur only in quality and not in nature) [BOURGES.2 ¶32]

«l’oeuf vitriolé qui n’exige plus qu’une cuisson graduée pour se transformer d’abord en soufre rouge, ensuite en Elixir, puis, au troisième période, en Médecine universelle.» (the vitriolated egg, which needs only a graduated cooking to be turned first into red sulphur, then into Elixir, then, in the third period, into Universal Medicine.) [PARIS.3 ¶18]

Identified in the text with: Philosophers’ stone, Tree of Life

Emerald of the Philosophers — Emeraude des Philosophes

term; Literal translation.

One of the names some Adepts gave to the Green Lion, with May Dew, Saturnian Herb and Vegetable Stone [PARIS.4 ¶17]. Arnold of Villanova (quoted) explains such green names: ‘our water’ takes the names of leaves, trees and all green things to deceive the foolish [PARIS.4 ¶17].

«d’autres, Emeraude des Philosophes, Rosée de mai, Herbe saturnienne, Pierre végétale, etc.» (others, Emerald of the Philosophers, May Dew, Saturnian Herb, Vegetable Stone, etc.) [PARIS.4 ¶17]

Encyclopédie — The Encyclopedia — Encyclopédie

work; Name as spelled in the text.

The eighteenth-century Encyclopédie is cited as a second proof that the Great Work can be done by two paths, the wet path, longer but more esteemed, and the dry path, less valued, in which the Celestial Salt, the Philosophers’ mercury, is cooked with an earthly metallic body in a crucible on a bare fire for four days [PARIS.7 ¶4].

«cuire le Sel céleste, qui est le mercure des Philosophes, avec un corps métallique terrestre, dans un creuset et à feu nu, pendant quatre jours» (to cook the Celestial Salt, which is the mercury of the Philosophers, with an earthly metallic body, in a crucible and on a bare fire, for four days) [PARIS.7 ¶4]

Entrée ouverte au Palais fermé du Roi — Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King — Entrée ouverte au Palais fermé du Roi

work; Name as spelled in the text (also ‘Entrée au Palais fermé du Roy’); English title is our literal translation.

Work of Philalèthe. The seventh Notre-Dame medallion (an old man entering the mysterious Palace) is ‘the Entrée au Palais fermé du Roy of Philalèthe’: the first step of practice, discovering the agent that reincrudates the fixed body [PARIS.5 ¶11]. Cited in Lenglet-Dufresnoy’s Histoire de la Philosophie Hermétique [PARIS.4 n1]. Canseliet: the book where he dwelt most on practice [PREF2 ¶33].

«C’est l’Entrée au Palais fermé du Roy, de Philalèthe, la première porte de Ripley et de Basile Valentin, qu’il faut savoir ouvrir.» (It is the Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King, of Philalethes, the first gate of Ripley and of Basil Valentine, which one must know how to open.) [PARIS.5 ¶11]

Epistola de Igne Philosophorum — Letter on the Fire of the Philosophers — Epistola de Igne Philosophorum

work; Title as spelled in the text, with our literal English.

A ‘petit ouvrage’ recommended, with Artephius and Pontanus, for precious indications on the nature of the secret fire [PARIS.3 ¶20]; a manuscript of the Bibliothèque nationale, no. 19969 [PARIS.3 n11].

«et du petit ouvrage intitulé : Epistola de Igne Philosophorum.» (and of the little work entitled: Letter on the Fire of the Philosophers.) [PARIS.3 ¶20]

Epistola de Lapidibus Philosophicis — Letter on the Philosophical Stones — Epistola de Lapidibus Philosophicis

work; Name as spelled in the text.

Haymon’s treatise, no. 192 in vol. VI of the Theatrum Chemicum (Argentorati, 1613) [AMIENS n7]; the source of the quoted passage on where to find the first agent [AMIENS ¶26].

«Haymon, Epistola de Lapidibus Philosophicis. Traité 192, t. VI du Theatrum Chemicum. Argentorati, 1613.» (Haymon, Epistola de Lapidibus Philosophicis. Treatise 192, vol. VI of the Theatrum Chemicum. Argentorati, 1613.) [AMIENS n7]

Escutcheon of Saint Thomas Aquinas — écusson de saint Thomas d’Aquin

place; Name as spelled in the text.

Hermetic coat of arms on a window of the chapel of Saint-Thomas-d’Aquin at the Jacobins’ convent, existing until the Revolution and drawn in 1787 by Chaudet [PARIS.8 ¶4; PARIS.8 ¶5]. Flamel’s version at the Innocents and Lagneau’s engraving derive from it [PARIS.8 ¶2; PARIS.8 ¶4]. Its blazon: an inverted gold matrass in a crown of thorns above; a gold cross with three blue globes and a red heart with a green branch, on which silver tears from the matrass gather and fix; a field of stars and rays, an earth with ears of corn, a violet cloud with three arrows, three silver serpents [PARIS.8 ¶6]. Fulcanelli says it reveals the secrets of extracting mercury and joining it with sulphur [PARIS.8 ¶7].

«Ce bel emblème a d’autant plus d’importance pour nous qu’il dévoile les secrets relatifs à l’extraction du mercure et à sa conjonction avec le soufre» (This fine emblem is all the more important to us because it unveils the secrets relating to the extraction of mercury and its conjunction with sulphur) [PARIS.8 ¶7]

Esotericism — ésotérisme

term; The usual English term; the text pairs it with exotérisme.

The hidden meaning of an image, opposed to its exoteric, public sense. Alchemy on Notre-Dame holds a closed book (esotericism) and an open one (exotericism) [PARIS.1 ¶3]; the cathedral’s teaching is outwardly lay and exoteric [INTRO.2 ¶4]. A careless restoration leaves ‘l’ésotérisme éteint’ [PARIS.7 ¶14]; the rose window’s glass lets ‘l’ésotérisme’ regain its purity [PARIS.5 ¶17]. Canseliet says Champagne’s plates wrap the book’s austere esotericism [PREF1 ¶12].

«tandis que la droite supporte deux livres, l’un fermé (ésotérisme), l’autre ouvert (exotérisme).» (while the right hand holds two books, one closed (esotericism), the other open (exotericism).) [PARIS.1 ¶3]

«mais le symbole en est tronqué, la science mutilée, la clef perdue, l’ésotérisme éteint.» (but its symbol is truncated, the science mutilated, the key lost, the esotericism extinguished.) [PARIS.7 ¶14]

Esprit Gobineau de Montluisant — Esprit Gobineau de Montluisant

person; Name as spelled in the text.

A predecessor in interpreting Notre-Dame, named with Cambriel [INTRO.2 ¶7]. His book, written on 20 May 1640, states that the king on the Saint-Anne pillar wears a triple crown [PARIS.7 ¶19]. Canseliet calls him a gentleman of Chartres and an eyewitness whose description proves the original statue stood on the middle pillar in 1640 [PREF3 ¶45, ¶46].

«Mais nous possédons, heureusement, le texte d’Esprit Gobineau de Montluisant, dont le livre fut écrit « le mercredy 20 de may 1640, veille de la glorieuse Ascension de Nostre Sauveur Jésus-Christ », et qui nous apprend positivement que le roi porte une triple couronne.» (But we fortunately have the text of Esprit Gobineau de Montluisant, whose book was written ‘on Wednesday 20 May 1640, eve of the glorious Ascension of Our Saviour Jesus Christ’, and which tells us positively that the king wears a triple crown.) [PARIS.7 ¶19]

«Après Gobineau de Montluisant, Cambriel et tutti quanti, nous allons entreprendre le pieux pèlerinage, parler aux pierres et les interroger.» (After Gobineau de Montluisant, Cambriel and tutti quanti, we shall undertake the pious pilgrimage, speak to the stones and question them.) [INTRO.2 ¶7]

Etteilla — Etteilla

person; Name as spelled in the text (also Eteilla).

Quoted on the Magi’s table: a book of gold leaves (the book of Thoth) and a vase full of celestial-astral liquor, where the secret lay [PARIS.1 ¶7; PARIS.1 n2]. He preserved the legends of a hermetic painting he is said to have owned (said to date from the mid-17th century), among them the advice not to rely too much on colour, which Fulcanelli calls worth following [PARIS.3 ¶33; PARIS.3 n13; PARIS.3 n14].

«Ne vous en rapportez point trop à la couleur.» (Do not rely too much on the colour.) [PARIS.3 ¶33]

«Le secret, le mystère étoit dans le vase.» (The secret, the mystery, was in the vase.) [PARIS.1 ¶7]

Eugène Canseliet — Eugène Canseliet

person; Name as spelled in the text.

Fulcanelli’s disciple and the writer of the prefaces: the first is signed ‘E. CANSELIET, F. C. H.’, October 1925 [PREF1 ¶13, PREF1 ¶14, PREF1 ¶15]; the third ‘Savignies, juillet 1964. Eugène CANSELIET’ [PREF3 ¶51]; the second is dated Savignies, August 1957 [PREF2 ¶46]. He received initiation orally from Fulcanelli, after Cyliani had ‘opened the door of the labyrinth’ to him in 1915 [PREF2 ¶4]; he wrote an Introduction to the Douze Clefs and a Preface to the Demeures philosophales [PREF2 ¶5], and a study on the Golden Fleece in Alchimie [PREF2 ¶20, PREF2 n1]. He met Fulcanelli ten years after Champagne did, i.e. 1915 [PREF3 ¶10].

«C’est, pour le disciple, une tâche ingrate et malaisée que la présentation d’une œuvre écrite par son propre Maître.» (For the disciple it is a thankless and awkward task to present a work written by his own Master.) [PREF1 ¶1]

Evangile selon saint Luc — Gospel according to Saint Luke — Evangile selon saint Luc

work; Title as spelled in the text, with our English rendering.

Cited (II, 1–7 as given) among the texts on the sign of the Nativity: shepherds in the fields at night, an angel, a divine light around them, the Good News, and the sign of a Child wrapped in swaddling clothes lying in a manger [INTRO.7 ¶19–22].

«et ceci sera pour vous le signe : vous trouverez un Enfant enveloppé de langes et couché dans une crèche.» (and this shall be the sign for you: you will find a Child wrapped in swaddling clothes and lying in a manger.) [INTRO.7 ¶21]

Evangile selon saint Matthieu — Gospel according to Saint Matthew — Evangile selon saint Matthieu

work; Title as spelled in the text, with our English rendering.

Cited (II, 1–11) on the Magi from the East who saw the star, which went before them until it stopped above the place where the Child was; they offered gold, incense and myrrh [INTRO.7 ¶23–28]. In the Hendaye chapter it is the Gospel of the age of iron: Matthæus is derived from the Greek for science, making it the Gospel according to Science, which teaches that, save a few elect, mankind must collectively perish; hence the angel attributed to Saint Matthew [HENDAYE ¶15].

«et voilà que l’étoile qu’ils avaient vue en Orient les précédait, jusqu’à ce qu’elle vînt et s’arrêtât au-dessus du lieu où était l’Enfant.» (and behold, the star they had seen in the East went before them, until it came and stopped above the place where the Child was.) [INTRO.7 ¶27]

«C’est l’Evangile selon la Science, le dernier de tous, mais le premier pour nous» (It is the Gospel according to Science, the last of all, but the first for us) [HENDAYE ¶15]

Evolution — Evolution

term; Literal translation.

The fifth figure of the Paris stylobate, which shows the oriflamme with three pennants, the threefold Colours of the Work [PARIS.3 ¶24]. The colours go from black to red through white [PARIS.3 ¶25]. The Green Lion is metallic youth ‘sur laquelle l’Evolution n’a pas ouvré’ but containing a latent germ [PARIS.4 ¶17]. Interpreted: Evolution names the progressive maturing of the matter, marked by the colours [PARIS.3 ¶24–25; PARIS.4 ¶17].

«L’Evolution succède et montre l’oriflamme aux trois pennons, triplicité des Couleurs de l’Œuvre, que l’on trouve décrites dans tous les ouvrages classiques» (Evolution follows and shows the oriflamme with three pennants, the threefold Colours of the Work, which are described in all the classic works) [PARIS.3 ¶24]

Explication très curieuse des Enigmes et Figures hiéroglyphiques — Very Curious Explanation of the Enigmas and Hieroglyphic Figures (of the great portal of Notre-Dame de Paris) — Explication très curieuse des Enigmes et Figures hiéroglyphiques

work; Title as spelled in the text (PREF3 spells it ‘tres-curieuse … hierogliphiques’), with a literal English rendering.

Work by Esprit Gobineau de Montluisant on the figures of the great portal of Notre-Dame de Paris [PARIS.7 n10], written 20 May 1640 [PARIS.7 ¶19]. Canseliet cites its description of the bishop putting his crosier in the dragon’s mouth, with a triple-crowned king in the waves, as proof against Grillot de Givry [PREF3 ¶45, ¶46].

«Explication très curieuse des Enigmes et Figures hiéroglyphiques, Physiques, qui sont au grand portail de l’Eglise Cathédrale et Métropolitaine de Notre-Dame de Paris.» (Very curious Explanation of the Enigmas and Hieroglyphic, Physical Figures which are on the great portal of the Cathedral and Metropolitan Church of Notre-Dame de Paris.) [PARIS.7 n10]

«Au pilier, qui est au milieu, et qui sépare les deux portes de ce Portail, est encore la figure d’un Evêque, lequel met sa Crosse dans la gueule d’un dragon» (On the pillar which is in the middle and separates the two doors of this Portal is also the figure of a Bishop, who puts his Crosier into the mouth of a dragon) [PREF3 ¶46]

Explications de l’Enigme trouvée à un pilier de l’Eglise Notre-Dame de Paris — Explanations of the Enigma Found on a Pillar of Notre-Dame de Paris — Explications de l’Enigme trouvée à un pilier de l’Eglise Notre-Dame de Paris

work; Name as spelled in the text.

Work of De Laborde, Paris, 1636, on the emblem of the Saint Anne pillar [PARIS.7 n8; PARIS.7 ¶11].

«De Laborde, Explications de l’Enigme trouvée à un pilier de l’Eglise Notre-Dame de Paris. Paris, 1636.» (De Laborde, Explanations of the Enigma Found on a Pillar of the Church of Notre-Dame de Paris. Paris, 1636.) [PARIS.7 n8]

Ezekiel — Ezéchiel

person; Name as spelled in the text.

Several authors read the Amiens wheel-fire as Ezekiel’s prophecy: he saw four winged creatures and wheels within each other [AMIENS ¶4]; Fulcanelli rejects this, since the figure has open eyes, no cloud border, and does not face the phenomenon [AMIENS ¶5; AMIENS ¶6]. In the Hendaye chapter Ezekiel’s vision of the four creatures with faces of man, lion, ox and eagle is quoted as agreeing with saint John’s [HENDAYE ¶13].

«en ont découvert le sens dans la prophétie d’Ezéchiel, lequel, dit G. Durand, « vit quatre animaux ailés, comme plus tard saint Jean, puis des roues l’une dans l’autre.» (found its meaning in the prophecy of Ezekiel, who, says G. Durand, ’saw four winged creatures, as saint John later did, then wheels one within the other.) [AMIENS ¶4]

F

Feast of Fools — Fête des Fous

term; The usual English term; the text gives the alternative name Fête des Sages, Feast of the Wise [INTRO.1 ¶11].

A medieval ceremony held in the cathedral, called also Feast of the Wise; Fulcanelli calls it a hermetic processional fair and a satire of an ignorant clergy subject to ‘disguised Science’ [INTRO.1 ¶11]. It had a chariot of the Triumph of Bacchus drawn by a centaur and centauress, Olympian deities at mass, and a mass composed by the initiate Pierre de Corbeil according to pagan ritual, with the bacchanal cry Evohé [INTRO.1 ¶11]. The Dijon Infantry was its last echo [INTRO.1 ¶15].

«Ce fut la Fête des Fous, — ou des Sages, — kermesse hermétique processionnelle, qui partait de l’église avec son pape, ses dignitaires, ses fervents, son peuple» (There was the Feast of Fools — or of the Wise — a hermetic processional fair, which set out from the church with its pope, its dignitaries, its devotees, its people) [INTRO.1 ¶11]

«Satire hilarante d’un clergé ignorant, soumis à l’autorité de la Science déguisée, écrasé sous le poids d’une indiscutable supériorité.» (Hilarious satire of an ignorant clergy, subject to the authority of disguised Science, crushed under the weight of an indisputable superiority.) [INTRO.1 ¶11]

Feast of the Ass — Fête de l’Ane

term; The usual English term; literal translation.

A ceremony almost as lavish as the Feast of Fools, with the triumphal entry of ‘master Aliboron’ into the church and an office exalting the asinine power that brought the Church the gold of Arabia and the incense and myrrh of Saba [INTRO.1 ¶14]. Fulcanelli reads it as a parody by the ‘mystifiers of the land of Saba, or Caba’, the cabalists, which the priest accepted without understanding [INTRO.1 ¶14]. Per Witkowski (QUOTED), a capital in the nave of Notre-Dame de Strasbourg shows it as the Procession of the Fox; an identical illuminated scene is in manuscript 5055 of the Bibliothèque nationale [INTRO.1 ¶14].

«Parodie grotesque que le prêtre, incapable de comprendre, acceptait en silence, le front courbé sous le ridicule, versé à pleins bords, par ces mystificateurs du pays de Saba, ou Caba, les cabalistes en personne !» (A grotesque parody that the priest, unable to understand, accepted in silence, head bowed under the ridicule poured out to the brim by these mystifiers of the land of Saba, or Caba, the cabalists in person!) [INTRO.1 ¶14]

«C’est la Procession du Renard ou de la Fête de l’Ane» (It is the Procession of the Fox, or of the Feast of the Ass) [INTRO.1 ¶14]

Figures symboliques d’Abraham le Juif — Symbolic Figures of Abraham the Jew — Figures symboliques d’Abraham le Juif

work; Title as spelled in the text, with our literal English.

Figures whose book, it is said, belonged to Nicolas Flamel, who kept them displayed in his scrivener’s shop [PARIS.2 ¶16]. The note cites a ‘Recueil de Sept Figures peintes’, Bibliothèque de l’Arsenal no. 3047 [PARIS.2 n7]. Fulcanelli quotes the descriptions of the third figure (a walled garden, an old hollow oak with a rose-bush of golden leaves and white and red roses, at whose foot a fountain bubbles which blind seekers cannot find, except one who weighs it in his hand) and the fourth (a red-clad king, soldiers killing children, a vat of blood in which sun and moon bathe) [PARIS.2 ¶17, ¶19]. The man weighing the fountain is the subject of the Notre-Dame relief [PARIS.2 ¶18]. A figure of Trismosin closely resembles the third [PARIS.2 ¶20]. The anonymous Clef du Cabinet hermétique says Flamel paints in these figures the hollow oak with its fountain, the oak signifying the oak barrel in which the water must be corrupted [PARIS.4 ¶14]. Abraham used the serpent on the golden rod ‘dans le livre qui échut à Flamel’ [PARIS.3 ¶17].

«Parmi les descriptions qui accompagnent les Figures symboliques d’Abraham le Juif, dont le livre, dit-on, appartint à Nicolas Flamel» (Among the descriptions accompanying the Symbolic Figures of Abraham the Jew, whose book, it is said, belonged to Nicolas Flamel) [PARIS.2 ¶16]

«Et au pied dudit creux de chesne bouillonne une fontaine clere comme argent, qui se va perdant en terre» (And at the foot of the said hollow oak bubbles a fountain clear as silver, which goes losing itself in the earth) [PARIS.2 ¶17]

First matter — matière première

term; The usual English term; literal translation of matière première.

The substance with which the Work begins. In the crucible it suffers the Passion like Christ, dies and rises again purified [INTRO.4 ¶2]; the cruciform church plan reveals its qualities and preparation [INTRO.4 ¶5]. The devil figure Maistre Pierre du Coignet represents the ‘matière initiale’ as Lucifer [INTRO.4 ¶6]. The Black Virgins figure it as the primitive earth the artist must choose, the first matter as ore, a black, heavy, brittle substance [INTRO.8 ¶4]. The Sages’ Chaos is their first matter, an elemental confusion [PARIS.2 ¶9]. Called Mirror of Art; this despised subject later becomes the Tree of Life, Elixir or Philosophers’ Stone [PARIS.5 ¶4]. It is the ‘sujet’ that contains the indispensable agent [AMIENS ¶27]; the dead trees of an Amiens quatrefoil refer to the ‘matières premières’, metallic planets killed by fire and made inert by fusion, like trees in winter [AMIENS ¶19, AMIENS ¶20]. Expressed by a book, open or closed [BOURGES.2 ¶16], and by the globe held by God, Jesus and monarchs [BOURGES.2 ¶27]. The text links it with the subject (sujet), Chaos and Mirror of Art.

«C’est la matière première à l’état de minerai, telle qu’elle sort des gîtes métallifères, profondément enfouie sous la masse rocheuse.» (It is the first matter in the state of ore, as it comes out of the metal-bearing deposits, deeply buried under the rock mass.) [INTRO.8 ¶4]

«Toutes les descriptions que nous ont laissées les Philosophes de leur sujet, ou matière première qui contient l’agent indispensable, sont fort confuses et très mystérieuses.» (All the descriptions the Philosophers have left us of their subject, or first matter which contains the indispensable agent, are very confused and most mysterious.) [AMIENS ¶27]

Identified in the text with: Mirror of the Art, Chaos

First stone — première pierre

term; Literal translation.

By the sign of the Cross the plan of the church reveals the qualities and preparation of the first matter, which leads to the ‘First stone’, cornerstone of the Great Work; on this stone Jesus built his Church, and the medieval freemasons followed the example; before being cut, the rough stone was often given the image of the devil [INTRO.4 ¶5]. At Notre-Dame this was the devil figure called Maistre Pierre du Coignet, symbol of ‘our cornerstone’ [INTRO.4 ¶6]. In the practice, after the second coagulation of the cock-fox operation ‘the first stone’ is born, neither wholly fixed nor wholly volatile, to be perfected by a third reiteration [AMIENS ¶15]. Canseliet: all truth, philosophy and religion rest on this ‘unique and sacred stone’ [PREF1 ¶6].

«ce qui aboutit, pour les alchimistes, à l’obtention de la Première pierre, pierre angulaire du Grand Œuvre philosophal.» (which leads, for the alchemists, to obtaining the First stone, cornerstone of the philosophical Great Work.) [INTRO.4 ¶5]

«Ainsi naîtra la première pierre, non absolument fixe ni absolument volatile, toutefois assez permanente au feu, très pénétrante et très fusible» (Thus will be born the first stone, neither absolutely fixed nor absolutely volatile, yet fairly permanent in the fire, very penetrating and very fusible) [AMIENS ¶15]

Fish — poisson

term; Literal translation.

Stated: the fish is the hieroglyph of the Philosophers’ stone in its first state, because the stone, like the fish, is born and lives in water [BOURGES.2 ¶14]; it is the fish ‘that swims in our philosophic sea’ of the Cosmopolite, to be caught with a net, image of the crossed lines on the galette [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. The Cosmopolite’s fish Echinéis leads to the pole star [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. A porcelain sole once replaced the baigneur in a cake: ‘sole’ from Latin Sol, the sun [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. The fish found in the cake is the ‘royal fish’; the dolphin, royal fish, gave the heir’s title Dauphin; the Greek Ichtus has the same origin [BOURGES.2 ¶15].

«Le poisson est l’hiéroglyphe de la pierre des Philosophes dans son premier état, parce que la pierre, comme le poisson, naît dans l’eau et vit dans l’eau.» (The fish is the hieroglyph of the Philosophers’ stone in its first state, because the stone, like the fish, is born in water and lives in water.) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]

«Au baigneur, on avait substitué un poisson en porcelaine, et ce poisson était une sole (lat. Sol, solis, le soleil).» (In place of the bather a porcelain fish had been put, and this fish was a sole (Lat. Sol, solis, the sun).) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]

Fish eyes — yeux de poisson

term; Literal translation; the quoted text glosses it itself (‘c’est-à-dire des petites bouteilles sur la superficie’) [PARIS.3 ¶11].

Stated (in the Clef du Cabinet hermétique, quoting Philalèthe): one sign of the first putrefaction is that the matter looks like fish eyes, that is, small bubbles on its surface, as if it were foaming, a mark that it ferments and boils; this fermentation is long and done by the secret fire [PARIS.3 ¶11].

«Philalèthe dit qu’il faut qu’elle paroisse comme des yeux de poisson, c’est-à-dire des petites bouteilles sur la superficie, et qu’il paroisse qu’elle écume» (Philalethes says it must look like fish eyes, that is, little bubbles on the surface, and that it should seem to foam) [PARIS.3 ¶11]

Flamboyant Gothic — Gothique flamboyant

term; The usual English term for this style.

Name given to the style of the 14th and 15th centuries; the text explains it by the predominance of the fiery symbol in the last period of medieval art, following its reading of rose windows as images of fire and of matter moved by fire [INTRO.6 ¶12].

«Dans l’architecture des XIVe et XVe siècles, la prépondérance du symbole igné, qui caractérise nettement la dernière période de l’art médiéval, a fait donner au style de cette époque le nom de Gothique flamboyant.» (In the architecture of the 14th and 15th centuries, the predominance of the fiery symbol, which clearly marks the last period of medieval art, caused the style of that time to be called Flamboyant Gothic.) [INTRO.6 ¶12]

Flogging of the Alleluia — Flagellation de l’Alleluia

term; Literal translation.

A yearly custom in Langres cathedral, among those in which ‘a hermetic sense, often very pure’ shows through: the choirboys drove their humming tops out of the nave with whips [INTRO.1 ¶15].

«comme la Flagellation de l’Alleluia, dans laquelle les enfants de chœur chassaient, à grands coups de fouet, leurs sabots ronflants hors des nefs de la cathédrale de Langres» (such as the Flogging of the Alleluia, in which the choirboys drove their humming tops with great lashes of the whip out of the naves of Langres cathedral) [INTRO.1 ¶15]

Foliated earth — terre feuillée

term; Literal translation.

Stated: the prepared mercury is a stony, friable, flaky mass whose crystalline plates lie like the leaves of a book; hence the names terre feuillée, terre des feuilles, livre aux feuillets [BOURGES.2 ¶16]. The Kings’ Cake’s flaky dough confirms this [BOURGES.2 ¶16]. The Cosmopolite says the weight of the water must be plural and that of the white or red foliated earth singular [PARIS.5 ¶6].

«Les lames cristallines qui en composent la substance se trouvent, en effet, superposées comme les feuillets d’un livre ; pour cette raison, elle a reçu l’épithète de terre feuillée, terre des feuilles, livre aux feuillets, etc.» (The crystalline plates composing its substance are indeed stacked like the leaves of a book; for this reason it received the epithet of foliated earth, earth of leaves, book of leaves, etc.) [BOURGES.2 ¶16]

«Le poids de l’eau, dit-il, doit estre pluriel, et celui de la terre feuillée blanche ou rouge doit estre singulier.» (The weight of the water, he says, must be plural, and that of the white or red foliated earth must be singular.) [PARIS.5 ¶6]

Forest of Morois — forêt de Morois

term; Name as spelled in the text.

The setting of the Tristan and Yseult corbel [BOURGES.1 ¶8]. Later the text calls it the forest ‘de Mort-Roi’, strange because the tree behind Tristan is laden with huge fruits, and would liken it to the Garden of the Hesperides [BOURGES.1 ¶10]. The text gives no further comment on the spelling Mort-Roi.

«Etrange forêt, en vérité, que celle de Mort-Roi, et combien nous serions porté à l’assimiler au fabuleux et mirifique Jardin des Hespérides !» (A strange forest indeed, that of Mort-Roi, and how inclined we would be to liken it to the fabulous and marvellous Garden of the Hesperides!) [BOURGES.1 ¶10]

Fountain of Life — Fontaine de Vie

term; Literal translation; the book itself renders the Latin ‘Fons Vitae’ of the Brixen inscription as ‘Fontaine de Vie’ [PARIS.2 n6].

The Brixen inscription has the blood of Christ and the Virgin’s milk mingle ‘et sit Fons Vitae’ [PARIS.2 ¶15]; the book’s French gives ‘la Fontaine de Vie et la Source du Bien’ [PARIS.2 n6]. The text says the esoteric tradition of the ‘Fontaine de Vie ou Fontaine de Jouvence’ was materialised in the sacred wells of medieval Gothic churches, whose water was held to heal [PARIS.2 ¶21]. In the Bourges chapter the text states why mercury is called the Fountain of Youth: when gold is dissolved in it to bring it back to its first state, ‘young’ or ‘child’ gold, the gold is reborn in its womb [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. Stated: the Fontaine de Jouvence is the philosophers’ mercury in which gold is rejuvenated [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. Interpreted: the name ties this entry to the occult fountain sculpted on the Paris portal (the dissolvent) [PARIS.2 ¶4–6].

«C’est la raison pour laquelle le mercure, — loyal serviteur et Scel de la terre, — est nommé Fontaine de Jouvence.» (That is why mercury, the loyal servant and Seal of the earth, is called the Fountain of Youth.) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]

«Disons enfin que la tradition ésotérique de la Fontaine de Vie ou Fontaine de Jouvence se trouve matérialisée dans les Puits sacrés» (Let us say finally that the esoteric tradition of the Fountain of Life or Fountain of Youth is found materialised in the Sacred Wells) [PARIS.2 ¶21]

Identified in the text with: Mercury

Four ages of the world — quatre âges du monde

term; Literal translation.

On the Hendaye pedestal a circle cut by two perpendicular diameters bears an A in each sector, the four ages of the world [HENDAYE ¶11]; the four great centuries, with their rulers Alexander, Augustus, Charlemagne, Louis XIV, figure them historically [HENDAYE ¶10]. In the Middle Ages the four phases are shown by the four evangelists or four animals around Christ [HENDAYE ¶12]. Hindu myth figures them by the cow of Virtue standing on four, three, two, then one foot: Crédayougam, Trédayougam, Touvabarayougam, Calyougam [HENDAYE ¶14]; ours is the iron age [HENDAYE ¶14–15].

«Ceux-ci sont chargés d’un A qui les désigne comme les quatre âges du monde, dans cet hiéroglyphe complet de l’univers, formé des signes conventionnels du ciel et de la terre» (These bear an A that marks them as the four ages of the world, in this complete hieroglyph of the universe, formed of the conventional signs of heaven and earth) [HENDAYE ¶11]

Fox — renard

term; Literal translation.

Stated: the fox, ‘notre renard hermétique’ or goupil, is the state of provisional fixity the volatile cock (mercury) must take when held long in the fire; then the water has become earth and the mercury sulphur [AMIENS ¶15]. This dry earth must be redissolved in its own water so the fox regains the wings and tail of the cock; repeated, the process yields the first stone, and a third reiteration is needed [AMIENS ¶15]. On the Paris stylobate a cock-fox monster shows the extraction of the red, incombustible Sulphur [PARIS.4 ¶19]; at Amiens the fox tries to reach the cock on the oak [AMIENS ¶14].

«Elle change alors d’emblème, et son endurance au feu, sa qualité pondéreuse lui font attribuer le renard comme enseigne de sa nouvelle nature.» (It then changes its emblem, and its endurance in the fire, its heavy quality, cause the fox to be given it as the sign of its new nature.) [AMIENS ¶15]

Fr. Noel — Fr. Noel

person; Name as spelled in the text (also ‘Noël’, PARIS.2 n3).

Author of the Dictionnaire de la Fable (Paris, Le Normant, 1801) [BOURGES.2 n9; PARIS.2 n3], cited on the Cista of Bacchus [BOURGES.2 ¶15] and as reference for the fountain Libéthra [PARIS.2 ¶7; PARIS.2 n3].

«dans laquelle, nous dit Fr. Noel, était renfermé ce qu’il y avait de plus mystérieux» (in which, Fr. Noel tells us, was enclosed what was most mysterious) [BOURGES.2 ¶15]

Franciscan convent of Cimiez — couvent franciscain de Cimiez

place; Name as spelled in the text.

Canseliet: one of the small alchemical paintings of this convent shows the two stars, with the Latin legend Cum luce salutem expressing the saving virtue of nocturnal starlight [PREF2 ¶23, PREF2 ¶24].

«Ne sont-ce pas ces deux étoiles que nous montre l’une des petites peintures alchimiques du couvent franciscain de Cimiez, accompagnée de la légende latine exprimant la vertu salvatrice inhérente au rayonnement nocturne et stellaire :» (Are these not the two stars shown us by one of the small alchemical paintings of the Franciscan convent of Cimiez, with the Latin legend expressing the saving virtue inherent in the nocturnal, starry radiance:) [PREF2 ¶23]

François Cambriel — François Cambriel

person; Name as spelled in the text (also L. P. François Cambriel, Louis-François Cambriel).

Author of the Cours de Philosophie hermétique (1843) [PARIS.7 n11]. He solved the enigma of the St Marcel pillar exactly, at least in part [PARIS.7 ¶17]; Fulcanelli’s reading broadly agrees with his, but rejects his extending the symbolism to the statue [PARIS.7 ¶28] and his claim that multiplication is not shown [PARIS.7 ¶23]. His engraving is called faked: bearded bishop, short crosier, finger to lips, a gesture ‘entirely from his excessive imagination’ [PARIS.7 ¶30, PARIS.7 ¶31, PARIS.7 ¶32, PARIS.7 ¶35]. Canseliet defends this verdict against Grillot de Givry and Jean Reyor [PREF3 ¶23, PREF3 ¶32, PREF3 ¶47, PREF3 ¶50].

«Cette énigme du travail alchimique, solutionnée d’une manière exacte, — au moins en partie, — par François Cambriel, lui valut d’être cité par Champfleury dans ses Excentriques, et par Tcherkapoff dans ses Fous littéraires.» (This enigma of the alchemical work, solved exactly, — at least in part, — by François Cambriel, earned him a mention by Champfleury in his Excentriques and by Tcherkapoff in his Fous littéraires.) [PARIS.7 ¶17]

«Quant au geste fameux des personnages du Mutus Liber et d’Harpocrate, il est sorti tout entier de l’imagination excessive de Cambriel.» (As for the famous gesture of the figures of the Mutus Liber and of Harpocrates, it came entirely from Cambriel’s excessive imagination.) [PARIS.7 ¶32]

François Rabelais — François Rabelais

person; Name as spelled in the text; also called ‘le bon curé de Meudon’ [INTRO.3 n1].

Author of the Life of Gargantua and Pantagruel, an esoteric work and a ‘roman d’argot’; the curé de Meudon shows himself a great initiate and a first-rank cabalist [INTRO.3 n1].

«Le bon curé de Meudon s’y révèle comme un grand initié doublé d’un cabaliste de premier ordre.» (The good curé of Meudon reveals himself there as a great initiate and a cabalist of the first order besides.) [INTRO.3 n1]

François Villon — François Villon

person; Name as spelled in the text.

The poet Villon was at the head of the truands of the Cour des Miracles, who, like the medieval Frimasons, expressed themselves in argot, as all Initiates did [INTRO.3 ¶5]. Canseliet’s third preface opens with lines from his Testament, stanzas XXXVI and XXXVII [PREF3 ¶1, PREF3 ¶8, PREF3 ¶9].

«Tous les Initiés s’exprimaient en argot, aussi bien les truands de la Cour des Miracles, — le poète Villon à leur tête, — que les Frimasons, ou francs-maçons du moyen âge» (All the Initiates expressed themselves in argot, the vagabonds of the Court of Miracles, — the poet Villon at their head, — as much as the Frimasons, or freemasons of the Middle Ages) [INTRO.3 ¶5]

Freemasons — francs-maçons

term; Literal translation; the text also uses ‘Frimasons’.

The medieval Frimasons or freemasons, ‘logeurs du bon Dieu’, built the argotic masterpieces, spoke argot like all Initiates, and knew the road to the Garden of the Hesperides [INTRO.3 ¶5]. They followed symbolically the divine example of building on the stone [INTRO.4 ¶5]. The cathedrals were built by them to transmit hermetic symbols and doctrine [PARIS.8 ¶1]. Their ‘Lost Word’ is the Verbum dimissum [PARIS.6 ¶25]. Canseliet: Freemasonry still seeks it [PREF3 ¶14].

«Edifiées par les Frimasons médiévaux pour assurer la transmission des symboles et de la doctrine hermétique, nos grandes cathédrales exercèrent, dès leur apparition, une influence marquée sur nombre de spécimens plus modestes de l’architecture civile ou religieuse.» (Built by the medieval Frimasons to ensure the transmission of the hermetic symbols and doctrine, our great cathedrals exerted from their appearance a marked influence on many humbler examples of civil or religious architecture.) [PARIS.8 ¶1]

Frédéric Portal — Frédéric Portal

person; Name as spelled in the text.

Author of Des Couleurs Symboliques (1857) [PARIS.3 n12], cited for the language of colours united to religion [PARIS.3 ¶26]. Within Pierre Dujols’s quoted passage, Portal says the black Osiris at the beginning of the funerary Ritual represents the soul passing from night to day, death to life [PARIS.3 ¶28].

«L’Osiris noir, qui paraît au commencement du Rituel funéraire, dit Portal, représente cet état de l’âme qui passe de la nuit au jour, de la mort à la vie.» (The black Osiris, who appears at the beginning of the funerary Ritual, says Portal, represents that state of the soul passing from night to day, from death to life.) [PARIS.3 ¶28]

Fulcanelli — Fulcanelli

person; Name as spelled in the text (also misprinted ‘Fulcanellli’ [PREF2 ¶3]).

The author of the book, Canseliet’s Master [PREF1 ¶1, PREF1 ¶5]. Canseliet: he ‘is no more’; he disappeared when the Sign was accomplished [PREF1 ¶4, PREF1 ¶5]; in 1922 he had not yet received the Gift of God but was close to it, and kept anonymity [PREF2 ¶1]; the name is a pseudonym ‘wanted by the Tradition’ [PREF2 ¶2]; he received initiation orally [PREF2 ¶4]; Basile Valentin was his first initiator, an anonymous letter-writer his true one [PREF2 ¶5, PREF2 ¶6]; he knew Julien Champagne in 1905 [PREF3 ¶10]. The epigraph signed FULCANELLI [PREF2 ¶51, PREF2 ¶52] repeats the author’s own words on dissecting words [INTRO.3 ¶7]. In the main text he says the problem of the secret fire stopped him for more than twenty years [PARIS.3 ¶20].

«Que de merveilles, que de choses insoupçonnées ne découvririons-nous pas si nous savions disséquer les mots, en briser l’écorce et libérer l’esprit, divine lumière qu’ils renferment !» (What wonders, what unsuspected things would we not discover if we knew how to dissect words, break their bark and free the spirit, the divine light they enclose!) [INTRO.3 ¶7]

«Fulcanelli n’est plus.» (Fulcanelli is no more.) [PREF1 ¶5]

G

G. J. Witkowski — G. J. Witkowski

person; Name as spelled in the text.

Author of L’Art profane à l’Eglise, quoted repeatedly as a source on church imagery (QUOTED: Witkowski): the satirical procession on a capital at Strasbourg [INTRO.1 ¶14]; the window of the Conception of Saint Romain at Saint-Jean, Rouen, where a star on the coverlet figured conception [INTRO.7 ¶36]; Notre-Dame-du-Pilier at Chartres [INTRO.8 ¶5]; a stone Isis at Saint-Etienne, Metz [INTRO.8 ¶13]; the Brixen painting of Christ’s blood and the Virgin’s milk [PARIS.2 ¶10].

«La conception était figurée par une étoile qui brillait sur la couverture en contact avec le ventre de la femme…» (The conception was figured by a star shining on the coverlet in contact with the woman’s belly…) [INTRO.7 ¶36]

«Cette figure en pierre d’Isis, écrit l’auteur, mesurant 0 m. 43 de haut sur 0 m. 29 de large, provenait du vieux cloître.» (This stone figure of Isis, writes the author, measuring 0.43 m high by 0.29 m wide, came from the old cloister.) [INTRO.8 ¶13]

Gabritius — Gabritius

person; Name as spelled in the text.

Named with Beya as the brother and sister whose union produces the compost or philosophical rebis, paralleled with Apollo and Diana [INTRO.3 n4].

«réalisé par l’union du frère et de la sœur, de Gabritius et de Beya, d’Apollon et de Diane.» (made by the union of the brother and the sister, of Gabritius and Beya, of Apollo and Diana.) [INTRO.3 n4]

Gall (oak-gall) — galle

term; Usual English for the oak growth; the text gives Latin galla.

The oak bears galls (noix de galle, Latin galla) [BOURGES.2 ¶22]. The text sets galla, Gallia, gallus side by side — gall, Gaul, cock — the cock being emblem of Gaul and attribute of Mercury [BOURGES.2 ¶22]. Stated: the gall gives the name of the raw mercurial matter, while the kermès names the prepared substance [BOURGES.2 ¶22]. The Golden Fleece hangs on the oak like gall and kermes [BOURGES.2 ¶23]. Greek gala means milk, and mercury is called Lait de Vierge [BOURGES.2 ¶23].

«Or, si nous rapprochons trois mots de la même famille latine : galla, Gallia, gallus, nous obtenons galle, Gaule, coq.» (Now, if we bring together three words of the same Latin family, galla, Gallia, gallus, we obtain gall, Gaul, cock.) [BOURGES.2 ¶22]

«Toutefois, tandis que la galle donne le nom de la matière mercurielle brute, le kermès (en arabe girmiz, qui teint en écarlate) caractérise la substance préparée.» (However, while the gall gives the name of the raw mercurial matter, the kermes (Arabic girmiz, which dyes scarlet) characterizes the prepared substance.) [BOURGES.2 ¶22]

Garden of the Hesperides — Jardin des Hespérides

term; The usual English term; literal translation.

The medieval builder ‘nautes’ knew its road [INTRO.3 ¶5]. Pegasus carried Perseus to the Hesperides [PARIS.5 ¶2]. The last Notre-Dame medallion shows the tree with the precious fruits of the Garden beside Jason and the ram [PARIS.5 ¶15]. Fulcanelli would liken the fruit-laden forest of Mort-Roi in the Tristan group to it [BOURGES.1 ¶10].

«Eux-mêmes, ces nautes constructeurs, connaissaient la route du Jardin des Hespérides…» (They themselves, these builder-sailors, knew the road to the Garden of the Hesperides…) [INTRO.3 ¶5]

«Etrange forêt, en vérité, que celle de Mort-Roi, et combien nous serions porté à l’assimiler au fabuleux et mirifique Jardin des Hespérides !» (A strange forest indeed, that of Mort-Roi, and how inclined we would be to liken it to the fabulous and wondrous Garden of the Hesperides!) [BOURGES.1 ¶10]

Gaston de Claves — Gaston de Claves

person; Name as spelled in the text.

Named only as the author to whom ‘others’ attribute the classic work (Le Filet d’Ariadne) of which Batsdorff is the presumed author [PARIS.3 ¶15; PARIS.3 n7].

«Batsdorff, auteur présumé d’un ouvrage classique, que d’autres attribuent à Gaston de Claves» (Batsdorff, presumed author of a classic work which others attribute to Gaston de Claves) [PARIS.3 ¶15]

Geber — Geber

person; Name as spelled in the text.

Huginus à Barma says the old masters, Geber, Lully and Paracelsus, each had his own process [PARIS.7 ¶3]. Geber is quoted praising the Most High for creating this Mercury, without which the alchemists’ work would be useless [AMIENS ¶15]. The threefold calcination of the Sulphur yields the three philosophical works ‘according to the doctrine of Geber’ [BOURGES.2 ¶32].
